## 导读

> 本导读整理自 Socratopia 网站本书介绍页：https://www.socratopia.app/library/close-looking-hokusai-en

You have seen The Great Wave so many times it has gone invisible: wallpaper, a logo, a thing your eye slides over. This book is built to get the gasp back, and it does it with one strange, freeing idea: a print has no original. The wave on your tote bag is one impression of an edition that ran into the thousands, and learning to ask which one (fresh or faded, an early all-blue pull or a late pink-boat state) turns out to be the single most useful habit a print-lover can have. No other kind of art makes you think this way; it is unique to the woodblock print, and it runs through all twenty-one chapters.

Each chapter is a followable act of looking at one work, or a small related cluster: the foam-claws of the Great Wave, the bare red cone of Red Fuji, the red lightning most people misremember as white, a cryptomeria so vast that three travelers join hands around its trunk while Mount Fuji shrinks to a thumbnail behind. You are taught to read the living, calligraphic line; the four graded blues a printer wiped onto the block by hand; the white of bare paper doing the work of spray and snow; and the three hands behind every sheet, the artist who drew it, the unnamed carver who cut it into cherry wood, and the unnamed printer who pulled it.

It is also honest in a way most appreciation books are not. Every visual detail here was checked against an actual high-resolution image of a named, specific impression; where a work could not be verified that way (the late ceiling paintings at Obuse), the book says so plainly and hands the looking back to you rather than faking it. It does not lecture. It stands beside you in front of the real sheet on your own screen, names which museum holds it, and points at the exact thing to find. The book contains no images of its own. It teaches you to see the real one.

By the end you can walk into any print room on Earth and read a Hokusai unaided, and you understand why this cheap Edo print, sold for the price of a bowl of noodles, ended up on Monet's wall at Giverny and on the cover of Debussy's La Mer . The book closes where it opens, on the wave that is still about to fall.

**Target audience**：Culturally curious, English-reading adults who love (or are newly curious about) Hokusai and want to understand what they are looking at, not just admire it. Also for travelers planning to seek out impressions in the world's print rooms, lifelong museum-goers, armchair lookers viewing on a screen, and Socratopia users who want a knowledgeable companion for one master in depth.

**Prerequisites**：None. No art-history or Japanese background is assumed. Every term (ukiyo-e, bokashi , karazuri , bero-ai / Prussian blue, impression, state, kachō-e , nikuhitsu , key-block, the three hands) is explained in plain words at first use, and every Japanese title is glossed in romaji, kanji, and literal English.

**Study hours**：~25–32 hours of companion-guided dialogue across 21 chapters, each self-contained within a single teaching window.

## Introduction

You have almost certainly seen _The Great Wave off Kanagawa_. It is one of the most reproduced images on earth, and that is exactly its problem: an image you have seen a thousand times becomes invisible. The eye slides over it. This book is an argument that you can get the seeing back, and not only for the wave but for a whole life's worth of pictures by the restless old man who made it.

Katsushika Hokusai (葛飾北斎, about 1760 to 1849) drew and painted for seven decades, changed his artist's name some thirty times, called himself "the old man mad about painting," and insisted that nothing he made before the age of seventy was worth counting. He left behind tens of thousands of images: the great Fuji prints, the waterfalls and bridges, the flower-and-bird sheets, the bottomless sketchbooks of the _Manga_ , and, at the very end, waves painted on temple ceilings and a tiger leaping through snow. This book does not try to survey all of that. It does something narrower and, we hope, more useful. It teaches you to _look_ at one work, or a small cluster of related works, per chapter, the way Hokusai and the two unnamed craftsmen beside him wanted you to look.

That phrase, "the two unnamed craftsmen," points to what makes this volume different from the others in the _Close Looking_ series. The earlier volumes treated single paintings: one canvas, one hand, one place you could go and stand before the original. A Hokusai print is not like that. It is a printed thing, made by three hands (the artist who drew the design, the carver who cut it into cherry wood, the printer who inked and pulled each sheet) and sold in editions of hundreds or thousands. There is no single original. The sheet you pull up on your screen tonight is _one impression_ among many, and a fresh early pull, with crisp lines and deep blue, is a genuinely different object from a faded late one. Learning to ask _which impression am I looking at?_ is the single most valuable habit this book can give you. It runs through every chapter and comes to a head on the last page.

A few practical things about how the book works.

**It contains no pictures.** The companion who teaches from it cannot show you an image, and neither can these pages. So every chapter directs your looking in precise words and then points you to a real, vetted impression you can open for yourself: a search instruction you can act on years from now, and, where one is stable, a direct pointer to a museum's own open-access scan. Open the work before you read, and keep it in front of you while you read. The Metropolitan Museum of Art, the British Museum, the Art Institute of Chicago, the Rijksmuseum, the Library of Congress, and Google Arts and Culture all publish superb, zoomable, freely viewable Hokusai images.

**Every visual claim has been checked.** Where a chapter states a specific detail as fact, that detail was verified against an actual high-resolution image of a named impression, or against the holding museum's own catalogue entry for that exact sheet. Where something could not be pinned down, or genuinely varies from one impression to the next, the book says so plainly and invites you to look rather than asserting. This honesty is not a hedge. It is the whole method. The point is to make the looking _trustworthy_.

**The Japanese is given accurately and glossed plainly.** No prior knowledge of art history or of Japan is assumed. Every term (ukiyo-e, _bokashi_ , _bero-ai_ , _kachō-e_ , _nikuhitsu_ , impression, state, key-block, and the rest) is explained in ordinary language the first time it appears, and every title is given in romaji and kanji with a literal English translation.

Each chapter ends with a set of **looking prompts** : not a quiz, but openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet, which your Socratopia companion can use to guide you. They ask you to find a particular detail, to trace where your eye is led, to compare two impressions and feel the difference, and to set one Hokusai beside another and watch what changes.

The chapters move, loosely, from the most famous icons through the breadth of the work to the very end of the life. You can read them in order, which is how the threads are designed to pay off, or you can open to whichever sheet you happen to be standing in front of. Either way, the goal is the same: that you walk into any print room or gallery on earth, or simply open a good scan tonight, and _see_. The wave is still about to fall. Let us go and look at it properly.

## Chapter 1: The Wave That Will Not Fall

You have seen this picture. It is on tote bags and phone cases, on the cover of a thousand paperbacks, tattooed on shoulders, printed on socks. You have seen it so many times that you have almost certainly stopped looking at it. That is the strange fate of the most famous image in the world: it becomes wallpaper. The wave that once made people gasp now slides past the eye like a logo.

This whole book is an argument that you can get the gasp back. Not by learning facts about the picture, though we will need a few, but by learning to _look_ at it the way the people who made it wanted you to look. And the very first thing to understand is that there is no single "it." The object we casually call _The Great Wave_ is not one painting hanging in one room where you could go and stand before the thing itself. It is a print, and a print has no original. There are something like a hundred and thirteen surviving sheets of the first edition scattered around the world, and the sheet you can pull up on your screen right now is one impression of an edition that ran, over years, into the thousands. No two are the same. Hold that thought. It is the secret at the center of this entire volume, and the wave is the perfect place to meet it.

So let us look. Find a good one first.

### 1.1 Where to find a good wave

Search for _Hokusai Great Wave Metropolitan Museum_ on Google Arts and Culture, or open the Met's own open-access page at metmuseum.org and search the accession number **JP1847**. That is the impression I am looking at as I write this, an early, well-printed sheet in the Metropolitan Museum of Art in New York (as of 2026-06, normally viewable on its open-access page; prints are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel). The Library of Congress holds a scan so large you can fall into it. The British Museum, the Art Institute of Chicago, the Rijksmuseum, and the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston all hold strong impressions too. Open one of them, full size, and put it next to this page.

The full title, the one printed in the box at the upper left of the sheet, is _Kanagawa-oki nami-ura_ (神奈川沖浪裏), which means, quite literally, "Under the Wave off Kanagawa." Not "the great wave." _Under_ it. The title is already telling you where to stand.

### 1.2 Where your eye goes, and where it is sent

Let your eye land wherever it wants. For most people it lands on the great claw at the top left, the crest of the breaking wave where the water has thrown up a fringe of foam that curls over into dozens of little reaching fingers. Good. Stay there a moment. Notice that the foam is not white paint laid on top of the picture. It is the bare paper. The printer inked everything around it and left those shapes untouched, so that the whitest thing in the picture is nothing at all, the blank sheet showing through. Already two hands besides the artist's are in play, but we will come back to them.

Now follow the wave. Its outer edge sweeps up from the lower left, rears to that overhanging crest, and the eye, having climbed it, is flung off the end of the curl and out into the open space at the right. And there, far away, small, sitting low in the trough between the swells just to the right of the picture's center, is Mount Fuji. Snow-capped, serene, the size of your thumbnail. The biggest thing in Japan, rendered smaller than the foam.

This is the whole composition in one move, and it is worth naming because Hokusai will do versions of it again and again. He has put a huge near thing and a tiny far thing in the same frame and made the small one the point. The wave is enormous and momentary; it will fall in the next second. Fuji is tiny and eternal; it has not moved in ten thousand years and will not move now. Your eye is pulled across the violence of the foreground and deposited, gently, on the still mountain. You have been _aimed_.

#### Definition: Composition

Composition is simply the arrangement of shapes within the frame, and the path that arrangement makes your eye travel.

In plain language: a designer cannot make you look at things in a particular order the way a writer controls the order of words. But a good one can stack the deck so heavily that you almost always look in the order they intend. Hokusai was, among other things, the greatest deck-stacker in the history of the print. He had trained partly on imported European pictures and their tricks of perspective, and he gave those tricks back transformed. The diagonal of the wave, the off-center placement of Fuji, the way the foam-claws point down at the boats: none of it is decoration. It is all traffic control for your eye.

Look once more and you will find the thing most people miss entirely. There are boats. Three of them, long and narrow, packed with men who have thrown themselves flat, crouching low along the hulls as the swells lift and drop them. These are fast delivery boats, the kind that raced fresh fish into the markets of Edo, the city we now call Tokyo. Two of them ride under the breaking wave; one is down in the central trough near Fuji. Find the rowers, pressed against the boards, and the picture changes. It stops being a pretty wave and becomes a moment of real danger, a dozen and more human beings about to be buried under water, with the indifferent mountain watching from miles away. (Count the boats and you will get three with no trouble. Count the men in each boat and you will find it harder; people often say eight to a boat, but at any normal resolution they blur into a single crouching mass, and I would rather you saw the mass than trusted a number.)

### 1.3 The blue

Now look at the color, because the color is half the miracle and it has a name and a history.

The deep, almost black-blue in the hollows of the wave, the mid-blue of the sea, the paler blue washing toward the horizon: this is not the soft indigo Japanese printers had used for centuries. It is a brand-new pigment, freshly arrived from Europe, called Prussian blue. The Japanese called it _bero-ai_ (ベロ藍), a contraction of "Berlin indigo." It was synthetic, intense, and unlike the vegetable blues it did not go muddy or brown. When Hokusai and his publisher got their hands on it around 1830, it was the hot new material, and the _Thirty-six Views of Mount Fuji_ , the series this wave belongs to, was in part a showcase for it. The series proved so popular that the publisher, a man named Nishimuraya Yohachi who ran a shop called Eijudō, eventually added ten further designs, bringing it to forty-six in all.

Look at how the blue is laid down. Along the horizon and in the sky, the color does not stop at a hard edge; it fades smoothly from deep to pale. That graded wash has a name too.

#### Definition: Bokashi

_Bokashi_ (ぼかし) is the printer's technique of graded color, a smooth transition from dark to light within a single printed area.

In plain language: woodblock printing works by inking a carved block and pressing paper onto it, which ought to give you a flat, even patch of one color. To get a _gradient_ instead, the printer wipes the ink across the block with a wet brush so it is thick on one side and thin on the other, then prints, fast, before it dries. Every sheet has to be re-wiped by hand. The four blues of this sea, deepening as the water curls toward you, are partly Hokusai's design and very much the printer's craft. No brush ever touched the paper to make that gradient. It was wiped onto a block and stamped, once per color, hundreds of times, each sheet a separate act of judgment.

This is the moment to say the thing that makes a print different from a painting, the thing this whole book is built around.

### 1.4 The three hands

When you stand before a Vermeer, you are looking at marks that Vermeer's own hand put on that exact surface. A Hokusai print is not like that. It is the work of at least three people, and to see it properly you have to see all three.

First, the **artist** (絵師, _eshi_), Hokusai, who drew the design in ink on thin paper. Second, the **carver** (彫師, _horishi_), an unnamed master who pasted that drawing face-down onto a block of cherry wood and cut away everything that was not a line, destroying Hokusai's drawing in the process, leaving a raised ridge of wood for every stroke. The crisp, living, calligraphic outline you admire, the taut edge of the wave, is as much the carver's achievement as the artist's; one slip of the knife and the line dies. Third, the **printer** (摺師, _surishi_), another unnamed master, who inked the blocks, registered each color to land in exactly the right place, wiped the bokashi gradients, and pulled each sheet by hand, rubbing the back of the paper with a pad called a _baren_. The depth of the blue, the softness of the sky, the precision with which the foam's white sits inside the blue without a hair's overlap: that is the printer's art. And behind all three stood the **publisher** (版元, _hanmoto_), Eijudō, who paid for the project, owned the blocks, and sold the sheets cheap, the price of a bowl of noodles, to ordinary people.

So when you say "a Hokusai," you are naming the designer and quietly forgetting two great craftsmen whose names history did not keep. Part of learning to look at these prints is learning to give them back their due: to read the carved line as carving, the printed color as printing.

#### Common Misconception

Many people assume that because there are many copies, every _Great Wave_ looks the same, the way every copy of a poster looks the same. The opposite is true. Each sheet was pulled by hand from wooden blocks that wore down with use, by a printer who re-mixed the inks and re-wiped the gradients every time. Early impressions, pulled when the blocks were fresh, have crisp lines and saturated, vivid blue. Later impressions, pulled after the blocks had worn and been recut, show softened or broken outlines and weaker color. The very last states of the _Great Wave_ are different objects altogether: the boats reprinted in pink, the distant clouds in graded brown, from blocks recut by other hands long after. There is no "the wave." There is _this_ wave, the one in front of you, and learning to ask _which one_ is the single most useful habit this book can give you.

### 1.5 Which impression are you looking at?

This is the question, and it is new to you if you have only ever loved paintings, so let us make it concrete.

Open the Met's JP1847 and the British Museum's impression side by side (search _Hokusai Great Wave British Museum_). They are the same design, cut from related blocks, and yet they are not identical twins. The saturation of the blue differs. The crispness of a given line differs. Scholars who have spent careers on this one design have identified several distinct printing states and at least seven different blocks across the long life of the edition, and they read the wear and the recuts the way a geologist reads rock. You do not need their expertise. You only need their habit: when you look at a print, ask whether the blues are deep and fresh or washed toward grey, whether the lines are sharp or soft. A fresh early pull and a faded late one are not two copies of one experience. They are two different experiences, and the difference is exactly the thing this book is teaching you to see.

The blue, remember, is _bero-ai_ , and Prussian blue is fugitive: light eats it. A sheet that hung in a sunny room for fifty years has a paler sea than the day it was printed. So when you find a _Great Wave_ whose water is a soft grey-blue, you are not seeing a worse picture. You are seeing an older one, a sheet that has lived a life. Knowing that turns a flaw into a biography.

### 1.6 The signature, and a man who could not sit still

Look back at that box of writing at the upper left, beside the title. The line of characters next to the cartouche is the signature, and it reads, in part, _Hokusai aratame Iitsu hitsu_ , which means roughly "from the brush of Hokusai, who has changed his name to Iitsu."

_Changed his name._ He did it some thirty times across his life. He was born around 1760, signed his early work under one studio name, shed it, took another, sold the rights to old names, adopted new ones to mark new phases. By the time of the _Great Wave_ he was in his early seventies and had recently become "Iitsu." Later he would call himself _Gakyō Rōjin Manji_ , "the old man mad about painting." He claimed that nothing he had made before the age of seventy was worth counting, that only at seventy-three had he begun to grasp the real structure of birds and animals and plants, and that if he could live to a hundred and ten, every dot and every stroke would finally be alive.

We will follow that restless old man through this whole book, because his story keeps changing how you see the work. But notice, even here, that the boast is not idle. The line in this print really is alive. The taut edge of the wave, the spring in the foam, the exact weight of each curling finger: this is the hand, and the carving knife serving the hand, of someone who had drawn water ten thousand times and was still not satisfied. Hold the signature in mind. It is the thread we will keep pulling.

One last thing before you go, because it reaches forward almost a hundred years and across an ocean. When the French composer Claude Debussy wrote his great orchestral seascape _La Mer_ in 1905, its published score appeared with this very wave on the cover. By then the sheet had crossed the world and helped remake European art and music. We will end this book on that story. For now, just register it: the wallpaper image on your phone case once detonated inside the imagination of Monet, of Van Gogh, of Debussy. You are looking at a small paper bomb.

Now look at it again. The claw at the top left, about to fall. The boats, the men flat against the boards. The little eternal mountain in the gap. The bare paper doing the work of spray. Three hands and a publisher, two hundred years ago, made this object so that you, tonight, on a screen they could not have imagined, would feel the water about to come down. It still has not fallen. It never will.

### 1.7 Looking Prompts

These are not quiz questions. They are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet. Open a good impression before you begin.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Find the three boats. Now find the rowers inside them, crouched low along the hulls. What does the picture become once you have seen the people in it?

Hint

Look in the troughs and under the curl of the big wave, not on the crests. The men are dark, low, and packed tight against the boards.

**Answer:** Before you find the boats, the wave is a beautiful abstract shape, all rhythm and curl. The moment you find the crouching men, the scale and the stakes snap into place: these are human beings about to be buried under tons of water, and the serene mountain is miles away and will not help. The picture turns from decoration into drama. This is Hokusai's quiet cruelty and his point, the tiny human caught between an indifferent sea and an indifferent mountain. The companion can use this to show how _finding the figure_ changes everything; it is a move we will repeat at Mishima Pass and the Sazai Hall, where again the real subject is small and easily missed.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Where does your eye land first? Now trace where it is sent next, and next. What in the design is steering you?

**Answer:** Most eyes land on the foam-claw at the upper left, then ride the wave's outer edge down and around, then get flung off the curl toward the right and drop onto the small Fuji in the trough. The steering is done by the great diagonal of the wave, the downward point of the foam-fingers, and the deliberate placement of Fuji just right of center so the wave's motion delivers you there. Notice that the most powerful shape in the picture (the wave) exists partly to hand you over to the quietest one (the mountain). That is composition as traffic control, the first of Hokusai's signature moves, and we will watch him use it for the rest of the book.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Open two collections' _Great Waves_ side by side, say the Met's (JP1847) and the British Museum's. Look for where they differ: the depth of the blue, the crispness of a line, the cloud at the horizon. Which one feels earlier?

**Answer:** There is no winner to crown here, and that is the lesson. Look for two qualities, saturation and sharpness. Deep, vivid blue with crisp, unbroken outlines suggests an earlier pull, taken when the blocks were fresh and the Prussian blue newly mixed; softer, greyer blue with any broken or doubled lines suggests a later or more faded sheet. If one of your two shows pink boats or brown clouds, it is a very late state from recut blocks, later still. What the companion is after is not a verdict but a habit of attention: get the learner to _notice the difference at all_ , and they will never again see a print as a flat, timeless thing.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this beside the picture you will meet in the next chapter, _Red Fuji_ : the same mountain, alone, bare, filling almost the whole sheet, with no wave and no boats at all. What does each picture want from Fuji, and how differently does each treat it?

**Answer:** Here Fuji is tiny, a still point glimpsed past chaos; in _Red Fuji_ it is everything, a single colossal cone with the sky behind it and not a human soul in sight. The _Great Wave_ uses Fuji as the calm at the eye of a storm, the destination your eye is steered toward. _Red Fuji_ removes the storm and asks whether the mountain alone, with almost nothing happening, can hold a whole picture. The fact that the same artist, in the same series, could make Fuji the smallest thing in one design and the only thing in the next tells you he was not painting a mountain. He was using a mountain. That realization is the hinge into Chapter 2.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the bare-paper foam. Tilt your screen or your imagination and notice that the whitest passages are not ink at all but the untouched sheet, the printer's restraint doing the work of spray. One thing the crowd misses: the boats and their crews, hidden in plain sight. And to view a good one now: the Library of Congress scan (search _Library of Congress Kanagawa oki nami ura_) is among the highest-resolution open files anywhere; the Met (JP1847), the British Museum, the Art Institute of Chicago, and the Rijksmuseum all publish strong, zoomable, open-access impressions. Whichever you open, pause before you admire it and put the first question first: whose sheet is this, and how fresh has its blue stayed?

### Chapter Summary

We began with the most familiar image in the world and tried to make it strange again. Along the way we picked up the whole toolkit this book will use. We learned to **follow the eye-path** a designer builds (the wave that delivers you to Fuji), the first of Hokusai's signature moves. We met the **bare paper** working as foam, the **bero-ai** Prussian blue and its smooth **bokashi** gradients, and the fact that the blue fades, so that an old sheet is a different experience from a fresh one. Most importantly, we learned that a print is made by **three hands** (the artist, the unnamed carver, the unnamed printer) and a publisher, and that there is no single "original," only _this impression_ among many. That last idea, summed up in the question _which one are you looking at?_ , is the discipline we will carry into every chapter ahead. In Chapter 2 we strip almost everything away and ask whether the bare mountain, alone, can do what the wave did. Keep the _Great Wave_ in your mind's eye as we go; we will return to it, and to Debussy's score, on the very last page.

## Chapter 2: The Bare Red Mountain

Almost nothing is happening. After the _Great Wave_ , with its claw of foam about to crash down on three boatloads of terrified men, you open this sheet and feel the silence of it. One mountain. A few clouds. A band of sky. No water, no boats, no rowers, no crisis. Not a single human figure anywhere on the page. Where the wave gave you violence and rescue and a thumbnail-sized Fuji glimpsed past the chaos, this gives you the mountain and the mountain alone, a great smooth cone of red rising from the bottom edge and filling most of the sheet, with a streak of mackerel cloud and an empty sky behind it.

And it is one of the most beloved images Hokusai ever made. The same artist, the same series, the same publisher, the same year, and almost the opposite picture. In the _Great Wave_ he proved he could orchestrate a storm. Here he proves he can hold your attention with a single shape and a change of weather. This is the power of subtraction, and learning to feel it is the whole work of this chapter.

### 2.1 The picture is a weather report

Start with the name, because the name tells you what you are looking at, and the popular name hides it.

Everyone calls this print _Red Fuji_ , or in Japanese _Aka-Fuji_ (赤富士), which means exactly that, "Red Fuji." But that is the crowd's nickname, the name the picture earned by being unforgettable. The title Hokusai and his publisher actually gave it is _Gaifū kaisei_ (凱風快晴), and it means something quite different. _Gaifū_ is the gentle south wind of summer. _Kaisei_ is clear, fine weather after the air has settled. Put together, the title is roughly "South Wind, Clear Sky."

That is not the name of a mountain. It is the name of a _moment_. Hokusai has not painted Fuji; he has painted a particular morning, in late summer, when a warm south wind is blowing and the sky has cleared and, for a brief while, the bare upper slopes of the mountain catch the early light and glow a deep reddish color. Anyone who has spent time near Fuji knows this is real and rare, a thing you might wait days to see and that lasts an hour. The picture is a weather report. It is the mountain caught in the one passing condition that turns it red, and the title says so plainly, while the crowd, looking only at the color, renamed it for the color and forgot the wind.

Hold that in mind as you look, because it changes the whole feeling. You are not looking at a permanent, eternal Fuji here, the way the _Great Wave_ 's tiny mountain was eternal. You are looking at Fuji for the length of one clear summer dawn.

### 2.2 Where the eye goes when there is almost nothing to steer it

In the _Great Wave_ your eye was hurled around the page like a passenger. The diagonal of the wave threw you up to the crest, the foam-claws pointed you down, Fuji caught you in the trough. That was composition as traffic control, Hokusai stacking the deck so you looked in his order.

Here he does something close to the opposite, and it is just as deliberate. There is no traffic to control. The mountain simply _is_ , occupying the right and center of the sheet, its long right flank sloping down toward the lower-right corner, its left side rising more steeply toward the summit. Your eye lands on the great red mass because there is nothing else for it to land on, and then it climbs.

Let it climb. Run your eye up the smooth outer edge of the cone, the long right slope, all the way to the top. At the summit you will find the one place where the design lets in some intricacy: a scatter of white, the snow that survives the summer in the high gullies, drawn as pale veins and patches that fork down a little way from the peak into the red. That white is the bare paper again, the same trick as the wave's foam, the printer simply leaving the sheet untouched so that the snow is _nothing_ , the blank page showing through the red and blue around it. The whitest thing in the picture is, once more, the thing that was never printed at all.

Then your eye, having reached the top, has nowhere to go but out into the sky, and the sky is the second half of the picture.

### 2.3 Two clouds, two registers

Across the upper sky, behind and to the left of the summit, runs a long horizontal band of small white clouds. Look at them closely. They are not soft puffs. They are drawn as a tight, repeating pattern of little overlapping scales or flecks, the kind of high cloud English speakers call a "mackerel sky" and the Japanese, with the same instinct, name for fish scales. Row on row of small curved marks, ruled out across the blue. It is a strange, almost decorative way to draw cloud, and it does a quiet job: it gives the empty upper sky a texture and a rhythm, so the blankness does not go dead.

Now look at the blue behind those clouds, because the blue is doing the most beautiful technical work in the whole sheet, and it connects this picture straight back to the last one.

#### Definition: Bokashi (again, and at full strength)

You met _bokashi_ (ぼかし) in the _Great Wave_ : the printer's graded wash, dark fading smoothly to light within a single printed area, made by wiping the ink unevenly across the block with a wet brush and printing fast before it dried.

In plain language: in the wave, bokashi gave you the deepening blues of the sea. Here it gives you the entire sky. Look at the top edge of the sheet and the blue is at its deepest, almost the dark blue of the wave's hollows. Now travel down toward the horizon and watch the same blue grow paler and paler until, near the base of the mountain, it has all but emptied to the bare sheet. That smooth darkening from horizon to heaven is one continuous bokashi gradient, wiped by hand onto the sky block and stamped, once, for every single sheet of the edition. No brush ever touched the paper. The whole serene depth of that summer morning is a printer's wrist, repeated hundreds of times, each one a separate act of judgment. When you admire the sky here, you are admiring the printer as much as the designer.

And the blue itself, remember, is _bero-ai_ , the new Prussian blue from Europe that Hokusai's publisher was showing off across this whole series. Keep your habit from Chapter 1 alive: that blue fades in light. A sky that has gone pale and grey is not a worse print. It is an older one.

### 2.4 The base, and the foot of the mountain

Now drop your eye to the bottom of the sheet, to the foot of the cone where the red meets the world.

Along the base, the smooth red of the upper slopes breaks up into a dark, dense, stippled green, the forest that skirts the lower flanks of the real Fuji. Where the upper mountain is one clean unbroken color, the base is busy, mottled, textured with countless small marks of green, so that the mountain seems to rise _out of_ a wooded skirt and float clean above it. This contrast, the bare smooth cone over the busy green hem, is much of what gives the design its strange monumentality. The eye reads the textured base as near and detailed, the smooth red as vast and far, and the mountain swells.

Here is a thing worth knowing, because it touches the impression question we are building toward. Scholars who have studied the blocks of this print believe the green base was a part Hokusai's craftsmen kept adjusting, recutting the block over the life of the edition. So the texture of that green hem is one of the places where two impressions of _Red Fuji_ can quietly differ. We will come to that. For now, just see how much the busy green does for the calm red above it.

### 2.5 Red Fuji, or Pink Fuji?

We arrive at the heart of this chapter, and it is the same secret the _Great Wave_ taught, now at its purest.

In Chapter 1 you learned that a print has no single original, that every sheet is a separate physical object, and that a fresh early pull and a faded late one are two different experiences. _Red Fuji_ takes that lesson and makes it almost unbearable, because here the difference between two impressions is not subtle. It can change the entire mood of the picture, even its name.

There exists, among collectors and curators, a famous distinction between two kinds of impression of this design. The standard one, the one most people have seen, the one on the postcards, is the strong **Red** impression: a saturated, warm, brick-to-russet red across the upper slopes, a full deep-blue sky, the crisp green base. That is almost certainly the standard **Red** impression you will find at the pointers below. It is gorgeous, confident, loud in the best way.

But there is a rarer kind, prized by people who love this print most, often called the **"Pink Fuji."** In these impressions the upper slope is not a strong red at all but a pale, soft, faded rose, and the whole sheet is gentler, cooler, quieter. For a long time these pale sheets were thought of simply as the faded ones, sheets whose pigment had weakened with time. But a number of specialists now argue something more interesting: that these paler impressions may sit closer to what Hokusai first imagined, a more delicate Fuji, and that the bold strong-red version we think of as the "real" _Red Fuji_ may be the _later_ development, the picture turned up in volume as the edition went on and the printers reached for a stronger pigment.

The point for you, standing in front of your screen, is not to settle that scholarly question. It is to feel the stakes of it. A "Pink Fuji" and a "Red Fuji" are not a good copy and a bad copy of one image. They are two genuinely different pictures, two different mornings, two different moods, struck from related blocks. One is a quiet dawn; one is a blazing one. And the only way to know which you are looking at is to look and ask.

#### Why the red itself is uncertain

There is a further reason the color of any given slope is hard to pin down, and it is worth saying plainly so you read with the right humility.

The reddish-brown pigment used for the slope was often an iron-oxide red the Japanese called _benigara_ (紅殻), an earth color related to rust. Earth reds of this kind can shift over time and from sheet to sheet; on one impression a slope may read as a warm true red, on another as a duller russet or even a brown. So if you are looking at a _Red Fuji_ and find yourself wondering "is that slope really red, or is it more of a brown?", the honest answer is that the question has no single right answer across the whole edition. It depends on the pigment that day, on how the sheet has aged, on which impression sits before you. Do not let anyone, including yourself, insist on one true color. Let your eye report what _this_ sheet does, and know that the next sheet may do something else.

#### Common Misconception

Many people assume the pale, pinkish _Red Fujis_ are simply damaged or sun-bleached versions of the "real," strong-red picture, the way a faded poster is just a worn copy of a vivid one. It is not that simple. Some pale sheets are indeed faded. But the soft "Pink Fuji" is widely thought to stand close to Hokusai's own early conception, with the strong-red impression coming later. So when you meet a gentle rose-colored Fuji, do not pity it as a worn-out _Red Fuji_. You may be looking at something nearer the source than the famous postcard is. As ever, the right move is not to rank the two but to _notice that they are different_ , and to ask which one is in front of you.

### 2.6 What subtraction buys

Step back now and feel the whole sheet at once, the way you would across a room.

The _Great Wave_ dazzled you with incident: the breaking crest, the boats, the flung foam, the hidden men. _Red Fuji_ gives you almost none of that. One shape, one weather, one stretch of patterned cloud, one graded sky. And yet it is not empty. It is _concentrated_. By stripping away every distraction, Hokusai forces the whole charge of the picture onto the mountain itself, onto the pure red mass and the way it sits against the blue. There is nothing to look at but Fuji, and so you finally look at Fuji.

This is the lesson the _Great Wave_ could not teach, because the _Great Wave_ was busy proving how much a designer can orchestrate. _Red Fuji_ proves the opposite and equal thing: how much a designer can leave out. The same man who could stack a page with controlled chaos could also clear it almost to nothing and trust a single shape to hold you. That double mastery, the storm and the silence, is most of what makes this series great, and it is why these two prints sit at the front of the book.

### 2.7 Looking Prompts

These are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet, not quiz questions. Open a good impression first; the pointer is in the viewer's note below.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Travel up the long right slope of the mountain to the summit. What is the white at the top actually made of, and why does that connect this print to the foam of the _Great Wave_?

Hint

Ask what color the printer laid down to make the snow. Then ask whether he laid down any color there at all.

**Answer:** The white snow at the summit is not white ink or white paint. It is the bare, untouched paper, exactly like the foam of the _Great Wave_. The printer inked the red of the slope and the blue of the sky around those shapes and left the snow as blank sheet showing through. So the brightest thing in the picture, the snow on the highest mountain in Japan, is literally nothing, the page itself. Seeing this binds the two prints together: in both, Hokusai and his printer use the white of the paper as a positive element, spray in one, snow in the other, and trust the viewer's eye to read absence as brightness. The companion can use this to plant the "bare paper as white" idea firmly, since it returns again and again, in the falling water of the waterfall prints and the mist of the bridges.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Is your Fuji a strong, saturated red, or a pale, soft rose? Try to find a second impression online that sits at the other end, and put them side by side. How different are the two pictures, really?

**Answer:** This is the signature question of the whole volume, and _Red Fuji_ is where it bites hardest. If your slope is a warm brick-to-russet red under a full deep-blue sky, you are almost certainly looking at the standard strong **Red** impression, the postcard one. If it is a delicate, cooler rose, you may be looking at a so-called **"Pink Fuji."** The crucial thing to feel is that these are not a vivid copy and a faded copy of one image. A pale "Pink Fuji" is widely thought to stand close to Hokusai's original, gentler conception, with the bold strong-red version coming later in the edition. So the two sheets are two genuinely different experiences, a quiet dawn and a blazing one, struck from related blocks. The aim is not to declare one impression the true one. It is to let the learner feel the gap in the body, a quiet dawn set against a blazing one, because once you can tell a Pink Fuji from a Red one you will never again mistake a print for a single fixed thing.

**Q2.** Look at the slope and ask yourself: is that color red, or is it brown? Now ask whether that question even has one answer.

**Answer:** It does not, and that is the lesson. The slope was often printed in _benigara_ , an iron-oxide earth red related to rust, and earth reds shift with age and vary from sheet to sheet. On one impression the slope reads as a warm true red; on another, duller, more of a russet or a brown. So whether your Fuji is "red" or "brown" depends on the pigment that day, on how the sheet has aged, and on which impression you hold. The right answer is to report honestly what _your_ sheet does and to hold the judgment loosely, knowing the next sheet may do something else. This is the discipline at its purest: there is no single true color to memorize, only the work of looking at the one in front of you.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this beside the picture you meet next, _Black Fuji_ (_Rainstorm Beneath the Summit_): the same mountain, the same silhouette, but in storm and darkness instead of clear summer dawn. What does it mean that Hokusai made the two as a pair?

**Answer:** _Red Fuji_ and _Black Fuji_ are companion pieces, the same cone rendered twice in opposite weather. _Red Fuji_ is the south wind and the clear summer sky, the mountain glowing in calm morning light, not a figure in sight. _Black Fuji_ takes the identical silhouette and plunges its lower slopes into storm-dark, with lightning forking across them. Recognizing them as a pair changes how you see each: the calm of _Red Fuji_ feels more deliberate, more precious, once you know its twin is being struck by lightning. Hokusai is showing off the same compositional idea, a single great cone filling the sheet, and proving it can carry two completely different moods. The fact that he built the pair tells you, again, that he was not painting _a mountain_. He was using the mountain as an instrument, playing it in a major key here and a minor one next door. That realization is the hinge into Chapter 3.

**Q2.** Now reach back to Chapter 1. In the _Great Wave_ , Fuji is the smallest thing on the page, a thumbnail in the trough. Here it is the only thing, filling the sheet. Same artist, same series, same year. What is going on?

**Answer:** It tells you, as clearly as anything could, that Fuji is not Hokusai's subject in the way a portrait's sitter is its subject. In the _Great Wave_ he needed a tiny, still, eternal point to steer the eye toward past the violence, so he made Fuji small. Here he wanted to test whether a single bare shape and a change of weather could hold a whole picture, so he made Fuji everything. The mountain is a tool he sizes to the job. Across the _Thirty-six Views_ he will shrink it behind a giant tree, hide it in a reflection that does not match, glimpse it through stacked timber, and here, blow it up to fill the sheet. Learning to ask "what is Fuji _doing_ in this design" rather than "how accurately is Fuji drawn" is one of the most useful habits this series can give you.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: stand back from your screen and let the picture work as a single shape across a room, the way a passer-by would have seen it in a print-shop window. Most of the time we study these prints with our nose against the glass; _Red Fuji_ was built to be read whole, at a glance, as one great red triangle against the blue, and it is worth giving it that distant look.

One thing the crowd misses: the _wind_. Almost everyone calls this "Red Fuji" and stops at the color. But the title, _Gaifū kaisei_ , "South Wind, Clear Sky," tells you the picture is about a fleeting weather condition, the summer south wind and the cleared dawn that briefly turn the mountain red. Once you know that, the redness stops being a permanent fact about the mountain and becomes a passing event you are lucky to have caught.

To view a good one now (as of 2026-06): search "Hokusai South Wind Clear Sky Red Fuji" on Google Arts and Culture, or open the Metropolitan Museum of Art's open-access pages and search the term _36490_ , or the Cleveland Museum of Art and search the accession number _1930.189_. These hold strong, zoomable impressions of the standard **Red** sheet. Prints are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel, and remember that the impression you open is one of many; ask whether it is a bold Red or a soft Pink, and how fresh.

### Chapter Summary

We took the most famous mountain in Japan and let Hokusai blow it up to fill a sheet, and we learned that **subtraction is its own kind of power**. Where the _Great Wave_ of Chapter 1 buried Fuji as a thumbnail in a roaring sea, _Red Fuji_ clears the page of everything, no water, no boats, not one human figure, and trusts a single red cone, a band of mackerel cloud, and a graded blue sky to hold us. We deepened two of Hokusai's signature moves from Chapter 1: **the designer's composition** , here reduced almost to a single great shape, and **the blue** , here the pure _bokashi_ of an entire sky, wiped by hand for every sheet. We saw the **bare paper** doing the work again, this time as summit snow. And we pushed the volume's central discipline to its sharpest point with the **Pink Fuji versus Red Fuji** question: two genuinely different pictures struck from related blocks, the pale one perhaps closer to Hokusai's first idea, so that the right move is never to rank them but to ask _which one is in front of you_ and to hold even the color of the slope loosely, knowing _benigara_ red shifts from sheet to sheet. We also recovered the wind hidden in the title, the brief south-wind dawn that the crowd's nickname forgot. In Chapter 3 we meet this picture's literal twin, _Black Fuji_ , the same cone in storm and darkness, and go hunting for the lightning that almost everyone misremembers.

* * *

---

## Chapter 3: The Same Mountain, Struck by Lightning

Picture the mountain you just left. The bare red cone of the last chapter, filling its sheet, calm in the clear air of a fine morning. Now keep its exact shape, the same broad triangle leaning a little to the right, and put it under a different sky. Drain the warmth out of the light. Sink the lower half of the slopes into darkness. And somewhere down in that darkness, let something tear.

This is that picture. Hokusai drew the same Fuji twice, in the same series, in the same publisher's shop, and set the two sheets against each other on purpose. One is the mountain in good weather. The other is the mountain in a storm. They are a matched pair, and you cannot fully see either one until you have seen it beside the other. So we are going to look at the storm, and we are going to keep the fair-weather cone of Chapter 2 propped open in the corner of your eye the whole time, because the design only makes its point as half of a pair.

Find a good one first.

### 3.1 Where to find the storm

Search for _Hokusai Rainstorm Beneath the Summit_ , or for _Storm below Mount Fuji Met_ , and you will land on the right sheet from several directions at once. On Wikimedia Commons the master file is named, plainly, "Lightnings below the summit," and it is a clean, high-resolution scan you can fall into. The Metropolitan Museum of Art in New York holds a strong impression (search its open-access collection for _Storm below Mount Fuji_ , item 36492); so do the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston, the Art Institute of Chicago (accession 87008), and the Harvard Art Museums. Open one of them at full size and put it on the screen beside this page, as of 2026-06, normally viewable on its open-access page. Prints are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel.

You may have noticed already that the museums do not agree on what to call it. The Japanese title in the cartouche is _Sanka hakuu_ (山下白雨), which means something close to "a sudden white rainstorm below the mountain," a squall of the kind that breaks fast on a summer afternoon. The Met files it as "Storm below Mount Fuji." In English you will also meet it as "Rainstorm Beneath the Summit," and, on Wikimedia, simply as "Lightnings below the summit." Hold that loose cloud of titles lightly for now. We will come back to it, because the disagreement is telling you something about the picture.

### 3.2 Two skies, one mountain

Let your eye land where it wants, then walk it down the sheet from top to bottom, because the whole design is built on that descent.

Up top, the summit. White, snow-capped, serene, sitting in a band of calm dark-blue sky that fades smoothly across the upper sheet. If you only saw the top third of this print, you would think it a peaceful picture. The air up there is quiet. The blue is graded, deep and even, the same patient wash you met behind the mackerel clouds of the fair-weather cone.

Now the upper slopes, just below the summit. They are reddish-brown, warm, lit, the russet you remember from the red mountain. For a moment the two pictures are nearly the same picture. Same color, same calm, same proud snowy peak.

Then the light fails. Drop your eye lower and the slopes darken, and keep darkening, until the bottom of the mountain has plunged into a near-black so deep it reads almost as a silhouette. This is the move. The top of the mountain is in fair weather and the bottom of the mountain is in a storm, in the same instant, on the same cone. Hokusai has split his Fuji horizontally into two worlds: serenity above, violence below. The summit does not even know what is happening at its feet.

That split is the entire idea of the sheet, and it is worth saying out loud because it is so simple and so daring. He did not paint a stormy mountain. He painted a mountain so vast that its head is in the sunlight while its base is being torn apart by weather, and he trusted a flat triangle and a darkening of color to make you feel the scale of that. The fair-weather cone of the last chapter said the same thing by subtraction, by taking everything away until only the mountain was left. This one says it by division, by cutting the single mountain into two weathers.

### 3.3 Now find the red

Here is where almost everyone, including people who have loved this print for years, gets it wrong. Ask someone what happens in the dark lower slopes and they will tell you, confidently, that lightning strikes, a white bolt cracking down through the black. They remember a white zigzag. They have to, because that is what lightning is.

Go and look. The lightning is not white.

Down in the dark lower-right slopes, forking in jagged, near-abstract zigzags across the blackness, the lightning is **red**. Vermilion. A bright hot orange-red, branching like the bare twigs of a winter tree or the veins in a leaf, splitting and re-splitting as it runs down into the dark. It is one of the strangest and boldest color decisions in all of these prints, and it is hiding in plain sight, and the crowd walks right past it because the crowd already knows what color lightning is and does not bother to check.

So check. Trace the forks. They are not a single dramatic bolt but a delicate, almost decorative tangle of red lines, scattered low and to the right, threading through the near-black like cracks through dark glass. Once you have found them you cannot unsee them, and the picture reorganizes itself around them.

Why is the lightning red? Honestly, we can only look and wonder; the old man left no note explaining it. But look at what the choice does. A white bolt against black would have been a cheap thrill, a flashbulb. The red instead glows, like something burning low rather than flashing bright, and it ties the violence at the base to the warm russet of the slopes above, so the whole mountain reads as one heated, charged body rather than a calm peak with a special effect bolted on. It is the only truly bright accent in a design of browns and blacks and blues, and Hokusai spends it on the one thing you would expect to be white.

#### Common Misconception

The white things in this print are the snow on the summit and the curling clouds. The lightning is the red thing. If you find yourself "remembering" a white lightning bolt, you are remembering an idea of lightning, not this sheet. The Japanese title even has the word for "white" in it, _hakuu_ , white rain, which makes the trap worse: the white in the title is the squall of rain, not the lightning, but it nudges the careless eye toward expecting a white bolt. This is exactly the habit Chapter 1 set out to break. Looking is not confirming what you already know is there. It is finding what is actually on the sheet, which is very often not what the famous reputation of the image has told you to expect.

### 3.4 A landscape that is almost an abstraction

Step back now and stop reading it as a mountain for a moment. Read it as pure design, as flat shapes and a few colors, and you will see how modern and how graphic it is.

A great dark triangle. A band of even blue above it. A few white curling clouds drifting in at the middle left, with a thin sliver of green foothills tucked beneath them. And, low in the dark, that scatter of red branching lines. That is nearly the whole inventory. There is no foreground incident, no boats, no travelers, no figures of any kind, not one human being anywhere on the sheet. There is no narrative to follow. There is a shape, a color split, and an electric red accident in the corner.

This is composition stripped to its bones, doing exactly what the wave's diagonal did in Chapter 1, only with almost nothing. The contrast itself is the structure: calm against violent, light against dark, smooth blue against jagged red, the broad simple triangle against the fine tangle of the bolt. Your eye is held at the top by the bright snowy peak, then pulled down into the dark, then snagged and held by the red. Up, down, then caught. The picture moves you without a single figure to follow, on the strength of color and shape alone.

When European painters first saw sheets like this, eighty years later, this is part of what stunned them: that a landscape could be built out of flat zones of color and a bold graphic shape, that you did not need careful shading and a window-on-the-world realism to make a powerful picture. A near-black triangle, a red zigzag, a calm blue band. It looks forward, across an ocean and the better part of a century, to a great deal of what we now call modern.

### 3.5 The pairing is the point

So set the two cones side by side, finally, because that is how they were meant to be seen. The fair-weather mountain of Chapter 2 and the storm mountain of this one. Same broad triangle, same lean, same snowy summit, drawn by the same hand for the same series and the same publisher within a year or two of each other, around 1830 to 1832, sold from the same shop, Eijudō. They are companion pieces, deliberate opposites, and putting them together is not a clever trick of ours but the design's own logic finishing itself.

Notice what stays the same and what flips. The shape is constant. The mountain is constant. What changes is everything weather can change: the light, the color of the air, the mood, the time of day, the violence at the base. One mountain, two conditions. It is the same impulse that drove painters later to set up an easel and paint the same haystack or the same cathedral front at different hours, the discovery that the subject can sit still while the light does all the work, and that the difference between two pictures of one unchanging thing can become the whole subject. Hokusai got there first, with a flat triangle and a publisher's commercial print series, decades before any of them.

Which means the question this little diptych really asks is not "what does Mount Fuji look like." Hokusai already told you, in Chapter 1, that he was never painting a mountain. He was using one. Here he uses the same fixed shape twice to paint two opposite things, calm and catastrophe, and lets you feel the gap between them. The mountain is the constant against which he measures the weather. That is why you cannot read this sheet alone. Half of its meaning is hanging in the other room, in fair weather.

### 3.6 Which storm are you looking at?

Everything we have said about the design holds across the early impressions: the split slopes, the dark base, the red lightning, the calm blue band, the absence of figures. Those are decisions carved into the blocks, and they survive from sheet to sheet. But the _feel_ of your particular impression, that is another matter, and by now you know to ask.

How dark is the base of your mountain? In a fresh, early pull, the lower slopes go to a deep, dense, almost frightening black, and the split between the lit upper cone and the drowned base is sudden and dramatic. In a later or more tired impression, that black can come out thinner, greyer, less total, and the whole drama of the descent softens. How saturated is the red of the lightning? A crisp early sheet has a hot, clear vermilion that leaps out of the dark; a faded one can let it dull toward a quieter brick. And the blue of the upper sky, the same fugitive _bero-ai_ you learned to watch in the wave, will be deep and even in a good pull and washed paler in a sheet that has lived too long in the light.

So when you open the Met's storm beside the one on Wikimedia, do not ask which is correct. Ask which is darker, which red is hotter, which blue is fresher, and let the differences teach you, again, that you are holding one printed object among thousands and not a single fixed masterpiece. And remember the titles, which wander as much as the printings do. The same design is "Storm below Mount Fuji" at the Met, "Rainstorm Beneath the Summit" elsewhere, "Lightnings below the summit" on Wikimedia, _Sanka hakuu_ in the cartouche. The looseness of the name is honest. Nobody owns the one true title any more than anybody owns the one true impression. The thing in front of you is _a_ storm sheet, named one way by the people who happen to hold it, and your job is to look at it rather than to settle its name.

### 3.7 Looking Prompts

These are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet, not quiz questions. Open a good impression first, and if you can, open the fair-weather cone of Chapter 2 beside it.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Find the lightning. What color is it, actually, on the sheet in front of you? Trace where it forks.

Hint

Look low and to the right, down in the darkest part of the slopes, not up near the summit. You are looking for thin branching lines, not a single fat bolt.

**Answer:** It is red. Vermilion, an orange-red, forking in fine jagged zigzags through the near-black lower-right slopes, branching like winter twigs or veins. Almost everyone expects white and misremembers it as white, partly because they know what color lightning "should" be and partly because the title has the word "white" in it, _hakuu_ , white rain. But the white in this sheet is the snowy summit and the curling clouds; the lightning is the one hot red accent in a design of browns and blacks and blues. The companion can use this as a small, clean demonstration of the book's whole method: stop trusting the famous reputation of the image and go look at what is physically on the paper. Once a learner finds the red here, they start checking everything, which is exactly the habit we want.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Start at the very top of the mountain and walk your eye slowly down to the very bottom. What changes as you descend, and where does your eye finally stop?

**Answer:** The top is serene: a white snowy summit in a calm, evenly graded blue sky, fair weather, quiet air. Just below, the upper slopes are warm reddish-brown, lit, almost identical to the fair-weather cone of Chapter 2. Then the light fails and the slopes darken and keep darkening until the base has sunk into near-black. And down in that black, your eye snags and stops on the red lightning. So the eye-path is a fall: from calm bright peak, down through the warming russet, into the dark, and caught by the red. The design splits one mountain into two weathers, serenity at the head and violence at the foot, and makes you feel the whole height of the thing by walking you from one to the other. No figure is needed to lead you; the color split does all the steering.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Open the Met's storm sheet and the Wikimedia "Lightnings below the summit" side by side. Compare the blackness of the lower slopes, the heat of the red lightning, and the depth of the blue sky. Which feels like the earlier, fresher pull?

**Answer:** There is no single right answer, and that is the lesson again. A deep, dense, almost frightening black at the base, a hot clear vermilion lightning, and a saturated even blue sky all suggest an early, well-printed impression, pulled when the blocks were fresh and the _bero-ai_ newly mixed. A thinner, greyer base, a duller brick-red bolt, and a paler washed sky suggest a later or more faded sheet. The whole drama of this design lives in the contrast between the lit summit and the drowned base, so a sheet whose black has gone weak has lost much of its point, not because it is a worse object but because it is an older, tireder one. Teach the learner to see the difference at all, and they will never again treat a print as a flat, timeless thing.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this storm beside the fair-weather _Red Fuji_ of Chapter 2: the same broad triangular cone, the same snowy peak, the same hand and series and publisher, in opposite weather. What stays the same, what flips, and why did Hokusai make them as a pair?

**Answer:** What stays the same is the mountain itself: the broad triangle, the lean to the right, the snow-capped summit, even the warm russet of the upper slopes. What flips is everything weather touches: the calm clear sky becomes a charged dark one, the evenly lit cone splits into a sunlit head and a storm-drowned base, and a hot red lightning tears through the dark where the fair-weather sheet had only serene air. He made them as a matched pair because the meaning lives in the gap between them. One unchanging shape, two opposite conditions, the difference becoming the subject. It is the same realization that would later send European painters to paint one haystack or one cathedral at many hours of the day. The constant mountain measures the changing weather. Seen together, the two cones tell you, even more plainly than the wave did, that Hokusai was never painting Mount Fuji. He was using it.

**Q2.** Both this storm and the _Great Wave_ of Chapter 1 put violence against an indifferent calm. How does each one stage that contrast differently?

**Answer:** In the _Great Wave_ , violence and calm are two separate things in the frame: the enormous breaking wave in the foreground, the tiny serene Fuji far off in the trough, the storm here and the peace over there. In the storm sheet they are the _same_ thing, the one mountain, split top from bottom: the peak is the serene Fuji and the base is the catastrophe, in a single body. The wave puts calm and chaos side by side and lets you cross the gap; the storm folds them into one cone and lets you fall down it. Noticing that Hokusai solved the same problem two ways, by separation and by division, is the kind of seeing this book is training.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the red lightning, low and right, branching through the dark. Find it on your sheet and you have understood the whole picture. One thing the crowd misses: that the lightning is red at all; nearly everyone remembers it as a white bolt and never checks. And to view a good one now: the Wikimedia "Lightnings below the summit" scan is clean and large; the Met (search _Storm below Mount Fuji_ , item 36492), the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston, the Art Institute of Chicago (87008), and the Harvard Art Museums all publish strong open-access impressions, as of 2026-06. Whichever you open, ask whose it is and how fresh, and ask how dark the base has stayed, because that black is the heart of the design.

### Chapter Summary

We took the bare cone of Chapter 2 and put it under a storm, and found that Hokusai had drawn the same mountain twice on purpose, fair weather and foul, as a deliberate companion pair. We watched the single cone split horizontally into two worlds, a serene snowy summit in calm blue above and a near-black storm-drowned base below, with the warm russet slopes between them. And we corrected the crowd's memory: the lightning forking through the dark lower slopes is **red** , vermilion, not the white bolt everyone expects, the one hot accent in a design of browns and blacks and blues. That correction is the chapter's small lesson in the book's large method, first set out in Chapter 1: look at what is actually on the sheet, not at the reputation of the image. We saw the design as a near-abstraction, a triangle and a color split and an electric red tangle, looking forward to modern art; and we saw that its full meaning hangs in the next room, in its fair-weather twin, so that the pairing is the point. In Chapter 4 we stay inside the Thirty-six Views and watch Hokusai take on something even harder to put on paper than a storm: the wind. At Ejiri he paints an invisible force directly, scattering papers and stripping leaves from a tree while Fuji looks on, calm and far, the way the summit here looked on, untouched, above its own storm.

* * *

---

## Chapter 4: Painting the Wind

A gust hits the road, and you can see it coming. Not the air, of course. You cannot see air. But you can see everything the air is doing to the things it touches, and Hokusai has crowded his picture with those things so that the wind, which has no shape and no color, becomes the loudest presence in the sheet. A line of travelers bends double. White papers tear loose and fling up into the sky. A hat goes spinning off. A little tree leans and lets its leaves go. And there, off to the left, calm as a held breath, stands Mount Fuji, untouched, as if the storm were happening in a different world.

This is one of the most quietly astonishing things in the whole _Thirty-six Views_ , and it is easy to walk past, because at first glance it looks like a simple travel scene. Let us slow it down, because what Hokusai is attempting here is close to impossible: he is drawing a thing that cannot be drawn.

### 4.1 Where to find the wind

Search for _Hokusai Ejiri in Suruga Province Met_ on Google Arts and Culture, or open the Met's own page at metmuseum.org and search the accession number **JP2953** , the impression from the Henry L. Phillips Bequest of 1939. That is the sheet I am looking at as I write this, and the same image appears on Wikimedia Commons under the file name MET DP141046 (as of 2026-06; prints are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel). The Museum of Fine Arts in Boston, the Art Institute of Chicago, the Honolulu Museum, and the Tokyo Fuji Art Museum all hold impressions too. Open one, full size, and put it next to this page.

The title in the cartouche at the upper left is _Sunshū Ejiri_ (駿州江尻), "Ejiri in Suruga Province." Ejiri was a real place, a post-town on the Tōkaidō, the great highway that ran between Edo and Kyoto, sitting near the harbor of Shimizu on Suruga Bay. Travelers stopped there to rest, eat, change their straw sandals. It was an ordinary, busy, slightly mucky stretch of road. Hold on to that ordinariness. It is the stage Hokusai chose for a small miracle.

### 4.2 The problem: how do you draw a thing with no shape?

Stop a moment before you look further, and pose yourself the problem the way Hokusai had to. You are handed a brush and asked to make a picture of _wind_. Not a windmill, not a sail, not a flag. The wind itself. What do you draw?

You cannot draw the thing, because it has no edges, no surface, no color, nothing for a line to grip. This is the first deep lesson of the chapter, and it is the engine of the whole design: **you draw a wind by drawing what it does.** You draw the bend in a back, the fling of a sheet of paper, the lean of a tree, the loss of a hat. The wind lives entirely in its effects, and Hokusai fills the picture with effects until the cause is undeniable.

Now look, and watch the effects pile up.

Along the road, a knot of travelers struggles forward against the blast. They bend at the waist, lower their heads, turn their shoulders into it. One or two clutch at their clothes and at their hats. Their bodies are doing the thing your own body does when you walk into a hard wind off the water: they fold. You do not need a caption to know which way the air is moving. Every spine in the picture is a little weather instrument, pointing.

Above them, the sky has come alive. A scatter of white sheets of paper, torn from somebody's bundle, go tumbling up and away to the right, high across the empty sky. They are loose, fluttering, each one caught at a different angle, some flat, some folded by the air. And just to make the comedy complete, a round straw hat has pulled free and goes spinning off at the far upper right, chasing the papers, lost, gone, nobody will ever get it back.

Near the center of the sheet stands a single slender tree, and it too is leaning, bending away from the gust, shedding a small handful of leaves that blow off rightward with the papers. The whole landscape tilts one way. Bodies, papers, hat, tree, leaves: every movable thing in the picture has surrendered to the same invisible push, all of it streaming to the right, as if the air had reached in and combed the entire scene in one direction.

#### Definition: Negative space

Negative space is the empty area _around and between_ the things in a picture, the part where, on the face of it, nothing is happening.

In plain language: when you draw, you naturally think about the objects, the figures, the tree, the mountain. But the blank stretches between them are not nothing. A designer can make that emptiness carry weight, can make it _push_ or _pull_ , can make it part of the action. In _Ejiri_ the great pale expanse of sky takes up most of the sheet and looks, at first, like a backdrop. It is not a backdrop. It is the arena where the wind performs. The papers and the hat are flung across it precisely so that you read the emptiness as moving air. The blank paper, here, is doing the heaviest work in the picture: it is the wind's stage, and without all that vacant pale sky for things to fly through, there would be no sense of distance for them to fly _across_ , no room for the gust to breathe. This is the same trick you met in Chapter 1, where the whitest passages of the _Great Wave_ were not paint but the untouched sheet showing through. There the bare paper became foam. Here the bare paper becomes sky, and the sky becomes wind.

### 4.3 The line that holds the storm together

Look at the carved line now, because a picture this busy could easily fall into chaos, and it does not. It stays legible. Every figure reads, every paper is distinct, the tree is crisp against the pale ground.

Trace the outlines of the bending travelers. The line that draws a stooped back, a bent knee, an arm thrown up to save a hat, is doing two jobs at once. It describes a body, and it describes a force. A standing figure drawn with a straight, calm contour would tell you nothing about the air. These contours are all curve and tension, every one of them bowed in the same direction, so that the carved outline itself becomes a record of pressure. This is the living, calligraphic line you have been learning to read, the one that was as much the unnamed carver's achievement as Hokusai's, set here to a new task: not the taut edge of a wave, but the bent edge of a person leaning into weather.

And notice the papers. Each little white sheet is just a few cut strokes, an irregular four-sided shape tipped at its own angle, and yet together they make a legible arc sweeping up and off to the right. The carver had to keep each one separate and clean against the pale sky, with no overlap muddying the others, so that your eye reads them not as a smear but as individual things caught in mid-flight. Get close to your screen and you will see how economical each paper is, how few marks it takes to say "a loose sheet, tumbling." That economy is the whole _Manga_ sketchbook discipline at work, the thing we will look at directly much later in this book.

### 4.4 The decisive contrast: chaos here, calm there

Now do the thing the design is built to make you do. Having taken in all that windblown commotion, let your eye travel left, to Mount Fuji.

Fuji stands pale and low at the left of the sheet, a quiet snow-streaked cone under a cloudless patch of sky. Nothing about it is moving. No paper flies near it. No tree bends toward it. It sits outside the weather entirely, serene, unbothered, as though the gust that is wrecking the travelers' morning were a purely local affair beneath its notice.

This is the heart of the picture, and you have met its logic before. In the _Great Wave_ , the violence was all in the foreground, the towering water about to come down on the boats, and the small Fuji sat calm in the gap, untouched. Hokusai is doing the same thing here, setting wild foreground against still mountain, but he has changed the element. In Chapter 1 the violence was _water_. Here it is _air_. There the chaos crashed down on you in a single enormous shape; here it is scattered, light, almost funny, a morning's worth of small disasters. But the deep structure is the same: the eternal mountain holds still while the human world is tossed around in front of it. Hokusai found this contrast once and clearly loved it, because he kept finding new weathers to stage it in.

Why does the calm Fuji make the wind feel windier? Because contrast is how the eye measures. Put a still thing next to a moving thing and the movement reads as movement. If the whole sheet were chaos, your eye would have nothing to push against and the storm would flatten into mere busyness. The motionless mountain at the left is the fixed point that lets you feel everything else fly. It is the silence that makes the noise loud.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume that to show motion, a picture has to blur things, the way a photograph smears a fast-moving object. Hokusai does the opposite. Every paper, every leaf, every bent figure is drawn with a perfectly crisp, sharp outline. Nothing is blurred at all. The motion is not in any softening of the line; it is in the _arrangement_ , the shared direction, the angles, the way the still mountain anchors the flying things. Sharpness and motion are not enemies here. A clean line tipped at the right angle, repeated across the sheet, can carry more speed than any blur.

### 4.5 The human comedy

Spend a moment with the people, because the picture is fond of them, and so should you be.

There is a small, warm joke running through the whole scene. These are not heroes or saints or legendary figures. They are ordinary travelers having an ordinary bad moment on the road, the kind anyone who has ever fought a gust on an exposed stretch knows in the body: the sudden grab at the hat, the half-turn of the shoulder, the bundle that comes loose at the worst time. Somebody's papers are gone for good. Somebody else is clutching at a hat that is three seconds from going the same way. You can almost hear the yelp.

Hokusai liked this. Across his enormous output he returns again and again to people caught mid-fumble, undignified, alive. The post-town setting matters to that affection: Ejiri was a place of foot-traffic and small commerce, of people on the move with their belongings on their backs, exactly the population to get ambushed by a sea wind. The grandeur of Fuji and the indignity of a lost hat are in the same frame, and Hokusai gives equal care to both. That double attention, the sublime and the comic sharing a single sheet, is a great deal of why his work feels so human two centuries on.

### 4.6 Which impression are you looking at?

You hold one sheet of an edition that ran into the thousands, and the habit this book keeps drilling is to ask which one. With _Ejiri_ , the question is gentler than it will be in the next chapter, and worth understanding precisely.

Some of Hokusai's designs have dramatic, documented printing states, whole alternate versions, an all-blue early pull versus a later colored one. _Ejiri_ is not one of those. There is no famous "blue _Ejiri_ " or radically different state to hunt for here, and you should not go looking for one or imagine you have found it. What you _can_ see, sheet to sheet, are the quieter variations that every hand-pulled print carries: how warm or cool the sky reads, how the graded tone of the sky and the ground was wiped on a given day, how crisp the lines are, how much the Prussian blue has held its strength or faded toward grey. Open two impressions side by side and you will find these differences without trouble, in the tone of the empty sky above all, since that vast pale area is where the printer's wiping shows most.
