Ask, as always, whether the blues are fresh or washed-out and whether the outlines are sharp or softened, and you will be reading the life of your particular sheet, even without a dramatic state to anchor on.

### 4.7 Looking Prompts

These are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet. Open a good impression of _Ejiri_ before you begin.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Find the flying papers in the sky and try to count them. How many are there?

Hint

Look in the open sky to the right of the leaning tree, sweeping up toward the far upper corner. They are small white irregular shapes, each tipped at a different angle. Do not forget the hat, off on its own near the top right, which is not a paper.

**Answer:** Here is the honest truth, and it is part of the fun: the count is genuinely hard to pin down. Most viewers land on something like eight, give or take, but the sheets overlap, some are tiny, some are tipped nearly edge-on and almost vanish, and reasonable people disagree. That is fine. The point of the question is not the number, it is the looking. While you hunt for each paper, you are following the exact arc the wind carved through the sky, from where the sheets tore loose, near the travelers, up and away to the right. By the time you have argued yourself to a count, you have traced the whole path of the gust with your eye. The companion can lean into the difficulty here: the uncertainty is the lesson, because a viewer who has tried and failed to get a firm number has looked far harder than one who accepted "some papers" and moved on.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Pick any movable thing in the picture, a paper, a leaf, a bent figure, the hat, the tree, and ask which way it is leaning or flying. Do this for several things. What do you notice?

**Answer:** They all answer the same way. Every loose, bendable, throwable thing in the sheet is committed to one direction, streaming up and to the right, away from a wind that is coming from the right side of the picture. The bodies fold rightward, the tree leans rightward, the papers and leaves and hat all fly rightward. Hokusai has unified the entire scene under a single invisible force, and that unanimity is exactly how you read the wind: not from any one object but from all of them agreeing. Then comes the exception that proves the rule. One thing does not lean. Mount Fuji, off at the left, stands perfectly still while everything else is blown sideways. The whole picture is a sentence about motion, and the mountain is the full stop.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Open two collections' _Ejiri_ side by side, say the Met's (JP2953) and another holding's. Where do they differ, and where is the difference easiest to see?

**Answer:** Look first at the great empty sky, because that is where a hand-wiped print shows its day most plainly. One sheet's sky may read warmer or cooler, more evenly graded or more patchy, than the other's; the grass and ground tone can shift too. Then check the blue and the lines, as always: deeper, fresher blue and crisper outlines suggest an earlier, better pull; greyer, washed color and any softened line suggest a later or more faded sheet. What you should _not_ expect to find is a wholly different version, an all-blue _Ejiri_ or a dramatically recut state. Unlike the Kajikazawa you will meet in the next chapter, _Ejiri_ carries no famous documented state, so the right question is the subtler one: not "which state is this" but "how fresh, how well-printed, how faded is _this particular sheet_." Learning to enjoy that quieter kind of difference, in a design without a dramatic state to lean on, is its own small graduation.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold _Ejiri_ beside the _Great Wave_ from Chapter 1. Both put a calm, distant Fuji against a violent foreground. What is the same, and what has Hokusai changed?

**Answer:** The deep structure is identical: serene mountain, chaotic foreground, the small still Fuji standing apart from the turmoil that fills the rest of the sheet. In both, your sense of the calm depends on the chaos and the sense of chaos depends on the calm. What Hokusai changed is the element and the mood. The _Wave_ 's violence is water, a single towering mass about to fall, genuinely deadly, the men flat against the boards. _Ejiri_ 's violence is air, scattered and light and a little comic, a morning's worth of lost hats and runaway papers. The _Wave_ makes you hold your breath; _Ejiri_ almost makes you laugh. Seeing that one artist built the same powerful contrast twice, once in terror and once in comedy, tells you the contrast itself was a tool he reached for on purpose, not a one-off accident of the _Wave_. He had found a way to make a still mountain make a storm feel stormier, and he used it more than once.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the lost hat at the far upper right, spinning off on its own above the flying papers, the picture's funniest and most human touch, easy to miss because it has drifted so far from the people it belongs to. One thing the crowd misses: that the enormous pale sky is not empty backdrop but the working part of the picture, the very thing that makes the wind visible. And to view a good one now: the Met's JP2953 publishes a strong, zoomable, open-access image (search _Hokusai Ejiri in Suruga Province Met_ , accession JP2953); the MFA Boston, the Art Institute of Chicago, and the Tokyo Fuji Art Museum hold impressions too. Whichever you open, ask whose it is and how fresh, and look hard at the tone of that big sky.

### Chapter Summary

We watched Hokusai do something close to magic: paint the wind, a thing with no shape and no color, by painting only what it does. The lesson at the center was that **you draw an invisible force through its visible effects** , the bent backs, the flying papers, the leaning tree, the runaway hat, all streaming one way under a push you never see. We learned to read **negative space** , the great pale sky that looks like nothing and is in fact the wind's whole stage, an idea that reaches back to Chapter 1, where the bare paper of the _Great Wave_ became foam and here becomes moving air. And we met again Hokusai's favorite contrast, the **calm Fuji against the violent foreground** , first felt in the _Wave_ 's towering water and restaged here as comic, scattering wind, with the still mountain at the left serving as the fixed point that lets everything else fly. We also tuned the impression question to a softer key: _Ejiri_ has no dramatic documented state, so the thing to read is the quieter life of your own sheet, the tone of that big sky most of all. In Chapter 5 the impression question goes sharp again. We turn to _Kajikazawa in Kai Province_ and its two genuinely different states, including a first pull printed almost entirely in blue, the single clearest lesson this whole book can give you in why it matters which sheet you hold.

* * *

---

## Chapter 5: A Sheet of Pure Blue

Picture a print with no red in it. No green, no yellow, no warm buff sky, no flesh tone on the one human face. One pigment, and that pigment is blue. Not blue as a strong note among others, the way the sea is blue in the _Great Wave_ while the sky stays warm and the boats stay tan, but blue as the entire world of the picture, deep in the rocks, mid in the water, pale toward the far air, with white left where the paper was never touched. A fisherman, a cliff, a river, a distant mountain, all of it built from a single color wiped thick or thin across the blocks. That picture exists, Hokusai's own publisher printed it, and it is one of the most beautiful objects in the whole series. It also exists in a second version with a red jacket and green rocks, and those two sheets are not the same picture. This is the chapter where the question we have been circling since Chapter 1, _which impression are you looking at,_ stops being a habit and becomes the whole event.

So let us find the blue one first, because if you open the wrong sheet the lesson inverts.

### 5.1 Find both, and put them side by side

Search for _Hokusai Kajikazawa Met_ , or open the Metropolitan Museum's open-access page at metmuseum.org and look up the accession number **JP2581**. That sheet came to the Met from the Howard Mansfield Collection by the Rogers Fund in 1936; on Wikimedia Commons the same image carries the file label MET DP141085. It is the one I am looking at as I write, and it is the blue-dominant early state, the version printed essentially in one color (as of 2026-06, normally viewable on the Met's open-access page; these sheets are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel).

Now do the thing this chapter is built around. Find a _second_ Kajikazawa, a colored one, and open it beside the blue. Search the title plus a different holding, or just _Hokusai Kajikazawa colored_ , and look for an impression where the fisherman wears a **red** jacket, the rocks carry a wash that runs from green into yellow, and sometimes a streak of pink lies across the sky. Several museums hold one or the other; some hold both. When you have the all-blue sheet on one side of your screen and the red-and-green sheet on the other, you are ready, because the rest of this chapter is a comparison you can actually see.

The full title in the cartouche reads _Kōshū Kajikazawa_ (甲州石班沢), "Kajikazawa in Kai Province," a rocky spot on a fast river in the mountainous old province of Kai, west of Edo, in what is now Yamanashi. The series is the same _Thirty-six Views of Mount Fuji_ , the same publisher Eijudō, the same moment around 1830 to 1832 when Prussian blue was the exciting new material. Hold that last fact close. It is about to matter more than it ever has.

### 5.2 The blue is the subject

Look at the all-blue sheet, JP2581, and resist the urge to hunt for the figure right away. Just take in the field of color. The river occupies most of the picture, a broad sweep of water graded from a deep, almost ink-dark blue in the shadowed troughs to a pale, watery wash where it meets the far bank and the sky. The rocks of the promontory at the lower left are the darkest blue on the sheet, nearly black. The distant Mount Fuji at the upper right is the palest, a ghost of a triangle in faded blue with snow left as bare paper. Between those poles, dark rock and pale mountain, lies every gradation of one pigment.

This is what the catalogues mean by an _aizuri-e_ , and it is worth defining carefully because it sits at the center of everything this chapter teaches.

#### Definition: Aizuri-e

An _aizuri-e_ (藍摺絵) is a print made essentially in blue alone, the design carried almost entirely by shades of a single blue pigment rather than the usual multi-block palette of reds, greens, yellows, and blues.

In plain language: a normal full-color print of this era was built from a stack of blocks, one for each color, each pulled in turn so that red sat here, green there, blue in the water, the warm sky behind. An _aizuri-e_ throws that variety away on purpose. The printer works mostly with the blue, deepening it in some passages and thinning it almost to nothing in others, so that the entire range of the picture, from its blackest shadow to its faintest distance, is one color asked to do every job at once. The pigment doing that job here is _bero-ai_ , the synthetic Prussian blue we met under the _Great Wave_ in Chapter 1. The new color was so prized, so saleable, that publishers around 1830 issued whole prints in nothing else, partly as a fashion, partly as a showcase. Kajikazawa is the masterpiece of that fashion. The blue is not the color of the water. The blue is the picture.

Once you have seen it that way, a strange thing happens to your sense of what the sheet is _about_. A normal landscape print tells you it is about a fisherman, or a river, or a mountain, with color as description laid over the subject. This one quietly tells you it is about blue, with the fisherman and the river and the mountain as the occasion for an essay in a single pigment. That is a radical thing for a cheap commercial print to be, and it is exactly the kind of bet a great publisher would place on a hot new material.

### 5.3 The two triangles

Now find the man. He stands on the dark rocky point at the lower left, his body arched forward over the water, leaning out with the unmistakable posture of someone playing a line against a current. From his hands two taut lines run down and to the right, into the pale water at the lower right of the sheet. He is concentrating, his whole frame bent to the pull. Behind him and a little below, tucked into the shadow of the rock, sits a smaller crouching figure, an assistant or a boy, easy to miss on a first look because the dark blue of the rock nearly swallows him. Find that second figure; the picture is lonelier and stranger before you do, and more human after.

Look at the shape the standing man makes. His braced legs, his forward-leaning torso, and the wedge of dark rock he stands on together build a triangle, point upward, leaning slightly into the river. Now lift your eye across the water to the upper right, to that pale ghost of Fuji. It is a triangle too, snow-capped, the same lean, the same upward point, set across the river from the man like an answer to him.

This is the move at the heart of the design, and it is the cleanest example in the whole series of one of Hokusai's signature compositions. He has rhymed the human form with the mountain form. The fisherman, small and mortal, bent over his one morning's catch, makes the very shape of the great eternal mountain across the water. We met the opposite arrangement under the _Great Wave_ , where the huge near thing (the wave) and the tiny far thing (Fuji) were opposites in scale and meaning. Here the near figure and the far mountain are not opposites; they are echoes. The man is not dwarfed by Fuji so much as paired with it, his fleeting labor given, for one instant, the geometry of the everlasting. Hold the two triangles in your eye at once and the whole sheet locks into a single shape: man becomes mountain, mountain becomes man, across a field of blue.

This is composition as we defined it in Chapter 1, the arrangement of shapes and the path it sends your eye along. But where the _Great Wave_ used composition to deliver your eye from chaos to calm, Kajikazawa uses it to make a _visual rhyme_ , two triangles calling to each other across the water. Find the rhyme and you have found the picture.

#### Common Misconception

Many viewers, told that this is a fishing scene and that the lines run to cormorants, go looking on the sheet for the birds and are quietly puzzled when they cannot find them. Here is the honest situation. The catalogues say the lines run to cormorants, the trained diving birds that fishermen once used on rivers like this. But you will not find the birds on this sheet. The lines simply descend into the water at the lower right and end. So do not strain to see cormorants that are not drawn; that way lies the bad habit of seeing what you have been told instead of what is in front of you. Read the lines as the catalogue's account of _what the man is doing_ , and read the sheet for what it actually shows: two taut lines vanishing into pale blue water, the rest left to your knowledge of the scene.

### 5.4 Which sheet do you hold? The two states, at full force

Now the chapter's true business. Bring your two open impressions back together, the all-blue JP2581 and the colored sheet you found beside it, and look hard at the differences, because they are not differences of _condition_. They are differences of _state_.

In Chapter 1 we learned that no two impressions of a print are identical: blocks wear, blues fade, an old sheet is a different experience from a fresh one. That was about the slow drift of a single design across hundreds of pullings. A _state_ is something sharper. A state is a deliberate change to the printing itself, a block added, a color introduced, the design reconceived between one batch of sheets and the next. Kajikazawa is the volume's clearest case because its two states are not subtle. They are two different visions of the same drawing.

The **first state** , the prized one, is the _aizuri-e_ you are looking at in JP2581: essentially all blue, the fisherman's jacket the same graded blue as everything else, the rocks blue, the world built from one pigment and its gradations. The **later state** keeps the same design but reintroduces color. The fisherman's jacket is printed **red**. The rocks below him carry a wash that runs from green into yellow, a _bokashi_ gradient laid across the stone. And in some impressions a streak of **pink** is added across the sky. The drawing is identical. The carved key-block outline is the same line. But the second sheet is warmer, busier, more conventional, more like every other print in the series, and the first sheet is austere, strange, and unforgettable, a single held note where the other plays a chord.

Which one is "the" Kajikazawa? Neither, and that is the lesson at full strength. They are two genuinely different objects. A person who has only ever seen the colored sheet has never seen the _aizuri-e_ that made this design famous, and a person who knows only the all-blue version is missing the warmer, more populated alternative the publisher also chose to sell. When you say you love Kajikazawa, the honest next question is _which Kajikazawa,_ and unlike the subtle fresh-versus-faded distinction of the _Great Wave_ , here the answer changes the picture's color, its mood, and arguably its subject. This is why this design sits at the center of the volume's impression discipline. It is the place where _which sheet do you hold_ is least deniable.

### 5.5 The printer's blue is the star

There is a second reason this chapter belongs at the heart of the book, and it concerns the three hands. Remember the division of labor from Chapter 1: the **artist** (絵師, _eshi_) draws the design, the **carver** (彫師, _horishi_) cuts the key-block and the color blocks, the **printer** (摺師, _surishi_) inks, registers, wipes the gradients, and pulls each sheet. In most prints you can feel all three at work, and the printer, however brilliant, is serving a color scheme the artist designed.

In an _aizuri-e_ the balance shifts. When you take away every other color, what is left is almost pure printing. The whole drama of the sheet, the deep near-black of the rocks, the mid-blue of the river, the pale wash of the far water, the ghost of Fuji fading to nothing at the top, is one pigment wiped at a dozen different strengths and graded smoothly between them by hand, sheet after sheet, with no second color to distract the eye or cover a fault. Every _bokashi_ gradient is exposed. The smooth fade from dark river to pale sky is not a happy accident of design; it is the printer's wrist, re-wiping the block wet for each pull, judging by eye how dark the trough and how pale the distance, getting it right hundreds of times. In a multi-color print the printer's gradients share the stage with the artist's red and the carver's line. In an _aizuri-e_ the printer's blue is the star, alone on it.

So when you admire JP2581, give the credit where the sheet earns it. The composition, the two triangles, the rhyme of man and mountain, that is the designer. The taut living outline of the fisherman's bent back and the lines from his hands, that is the carver, cutting a key-block that has to carry the whole picture with no color to hide behind. But the thing that makes this sheet _breathtaking_ rather than merely clever, the field of graded blue itself, is the printer's, an unnamed master wiping _bero-ai_ into a smooth descent from black to nothing. This is the chapter to learn to read the printing as printing, because here, for once, the printing has nowhere to hide.

### 5.6 Looking Prompts

These are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet. Open the all-blue JP2581 first, and have a colored impression open beside it before you begin.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Find the standing fisherman and trace the triangle his braced body and the rock make. Now find the distant Fuji at the upper right and trace its triangle. What does the design do by making these two shapes rhyme?

Hint

Look at the lean and the upward point of each shape, the man at lower left, the mountain at upper right, across the width of the river. They are not the same size, but they are the same shape.

**Answer:** The two triangles rhyme across the water: the small mortal man bent over his morning's fishing makes, for one instant, the very geometry of the eternal mountain. This is the opposite of the _Great Wave_ , where Fuji and the foreground were opposites of scale and meaning. Here they are echoes, and the rhyme quietly dignifies the fisherman, lending his fleeting labor the shape of the everlasting. The companion can use this to show how composition can _argue_ something, here a kinship between human work and the natural world, purely through repeated shape. Once a learner catches the rhyme, the whole sheet snaps shut around it into a single design.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Let your eye start on the dark rocks at the lower left, climb to the fisherman, follow the two taut lines down to the right where they vanish into the water, then lift across the pale river to Fuji. What carries you from the dark near corner to the pale far one?

**Answer:** The blue carries you. The eye-path runs from the darkest passage (the near rocks) along the figure and his lines, then across the graded water as it pales toward the distance, and arrives at the palest passage of all, the ghost-blue Fuji. The journey from foreground to background _is_ the journey from deep blue to pale blue, so the gradient does double duty: it builds the space, and it steers the eye. In a multi-color print other hues would interrupt that smooth descent. Stripped to one pigment, the path is pure and unbroken, near-dark to far-pale, rock to mountain. The companion can point out that the eye-path and the color-gradient are here the same thing, which is only possible because the sheet is all blue.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Put the all-blue JP2581 beside a colored impression where the fisherman wears red and the rocks run green-to-yellow. These are the same design. Are they the same picture? Which would you rather hold, and why?

**Answer:** They are not the same picture, and that is the heart of this chapter. The two are documented _states_ , not just a fresh and a faded copy of one thing. The first state is the _aizuri-e_ , all blue, austere, a single held note, the version that made this design famous. The later state reintroduces a red jacket, a green-to-yellow _bokashi_ on the rocks, sometimes a pink streak in the sky, and reads warmer, busier, more like every other print in the series. There is no correct preference, but there is a real difference, and the companion's job is to make the learner _feel_ it rather than rank it. A viewer who has seen only one of the two has not seen Kajikazawa whole. This is the volume's signature question at its sharpest: when you love this print, you must say _which_ one you love, because the color, the mood, and arguably the subject change between them.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this beside the blue of the _Great Wave_ from Chapter 1. Both lean hard on _bero-ai_ , Prussian blue. What does each design do with the blue, and what changes when blue is not one color among several but the only color there is?

**Answer:** In the _Great Wave_ , blue is the dominant and most thrilling color, but it shares the sheet with a warm buff sky and tan boats, and it is unmistakably _the color of the sea_ , description laid over a subject. In Kajikazawa's all-blue state, blue is no longer the color of anything in particular; it is the substance of the whole picture, rocks and river and mountain and man, the subject itself rather than a description of one. When a pigment is the only color present, it stops being an attribute of objects and becomes the medium of the entire image, and every gradation of it has to carry weight that color contrasts would otherwise share. The companion can use this pair to show the difference between blue as a brilliant note and blue as a complete world, and to make the point that the same new pigment, in the same series and the same year, could be a star among colors or the whole stage.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the smaller crouching figure tucked into the shadow of the rock behind and below the standing fisherman. He is easy to miss because the dark blue of the rock nearly swallows him, and the picture is lonelier before you find him. One thing the crowd misses: that there are _two_ Kajikazawas, and that most reproductions on mugs and posters show whichever the maker happened to find, so a person can love this image for years without knowing the other state exists. And to view good ones now: the Met's JP2581 (search _Hokusai Kajikazawa Met_) is a strong all-blue impression on an open-access, zoomable page; for the colored state, search the title with a different holding or simply _Hokusai Kajikazawa colored_ and compare. Whichever you open, ask first whether you are holding the _aizuri-e_ or the colored sheet, because here, more than anywhere in the series, that single question changes what you are looking at.

### Chapter Summary

We came to the print that turns the volume's central discipline up to full force. We named the **aizuri-e** , the all-blue picture, and saw the _bero-ai_ Prussian blue first met under the _Great Wave_ in Chapter 1 become not one color among several but the entire world of the sheet, graded from near-black rock to ghost-pale Fuji by the printer's wrist alone. We found the design's quiet argument, the **rhyme of the two triangles** , the fisherman's bent body echoing the distant mountain across the water, a deepening of the composition thread that began with the wave delivering the eye to Fuji. We were honest about the cormorants the catalogues name but the sheet does not show. And we met, at last unmissable, the volume's signature truth: this design exists in **two documented states** , the prized all-blue first pull and a later sheet with a red jacket and green-to-yellow rocks, two genuinely different objects, so that loving Kajikazawa means saying _which_ Kajikazawa. Chapters 2 and 3 stripped the mountain almost bare and asked whether subtraction could carry a picture; here subtraction goes further still, down to a single pigment. In Chapter 6 we go to the opposite extreme. At Mishima Pass the near thing becomes enormous, a single colossal cryptomeria so vast that three travelers must join hands to reach around its trunk, with Fuji shrunk to a pale shape behind it. Keep the two triangles in mind as we go, because the next chapter is about scale, and about what happens to a human being standing at the foot of something truly large.

* * *

---

## Chapter 6: The Tree That Dwarfs the Mountain

Three travelers are hugging a tree. They have spread their arms wide, leaned their bodies in against the bark, and joined hands around the trunk, and even so, with three of them stretched fingertip to fingertip, they cannot quite close the circle. The trunk is too fat. It goes up out of the top of the picture and down past their feet, a single brown column too wide for three men with arms spread to encircle, and it sits dead in the middle of the sheet like a pillar holding up the sky. Somewhere off to the right, small, pale, half-lost in a drift of cloud, is Mount Fuji.

That is the whole joke, and it is a real joke, and Hokusai means it. He has taken the most sacred, most enormous, most unmovable thing in his country and made it the second-biggest object in the picture. A tree beats the mountain. Find the impression and you will see that he is not being subtle about it: the eye goes to the men and their tree first, every time, and has to go hunting for Fuji afterward, the way you hunt for a friend in a crowd. This chapter is about scale, and about the small, funny, moving thing that happens when human beings stand next to something too big to take in and reach out to touch it anyway.

### 6.1 Where to find the men and the tree

Search for _Hokusai Mishima Pass Kai Province Met_ , or open the Met's open-access page at metmuseum.org and search the accession number **JP2970**. That is the sheet I have in front of me, an impression from the Henry L. Phillips Bequest of 1939 in the Metropolitan Museum of Art (as of 2026-06, normally viewable on its open-access page; like all these prints it is light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel). The same Wikimedia file, labeled MET DP140981, is the one I read closely for this chapter. If you want to compare, the Art Institute of Chicago holds a strong impression (accession 24786), and so do Yale, the British Museum, and the Library of Congress. Open one full size and put it beside this page.

The title in the cartouche reads _Kōshū Mishima-goe_ (甲州三嶌越), which means "Mishima Pass in Kai Province." Kai is the old name for the mountainous region west of Edo, now Yamanashi, and Mishima Pass was a high road that travelers crossed on the way through. So everyone in this picture is on a journey. They have stopped, partway, to do something that pilgrims and porters and ordinary walkers have done in front of great trees for as long as there have been great trees: they have stopped to measure it with their bodies.

### 6.2 The body as a ruler

Look at what the three men are actually doing. This is the heart of the sheet, so stay here a while.

They are not resting against the tree. They are spanning it. One stands with his back nearly to us, arms thrown wide; the next leans in from the side; the third reaches around from the far edge, and their hands meet, or nearly meet, in a human chain wrapped around the bark. They are taking the measure of the trunk the only way a person without a tape can: by becoming the tape. Arm-span to arm-span, they are asking _how big is this, really_ , and the answer their own bodies give them is _bigger than three of us_.

There is something I find genuinely touching about this, and it is worth slowing down to name. Numbers would not do it. If a sign said the tree was so many feet around, you would read the figure and feel nothing. But three men with their cheeks against the bark and their fingers straining to touch, that you feel in your own shoulders. Hokusai has found the one gesture that turns size into sensation. He makes you understand the tree not by telling you its dimensions but by showing you human beings using themselves as the unit of measurement and coming up short. The trunk is rendered, on the named sheet, in long vertical striations, the bark's grain running straight up the column, so that even the surface of the tree seems to stream upward past the men and out of the frame. They are small horizontal creatures clinging to a great vertical fact.

They are not alone on the road. Let your eye move out from the trunk and you will find more travelers nearby. On the named impression there is a figure off to one side wearing a wide conical hat and carrying a load, the standard silhouette of someone walking a long way with their life on their back, and another crouched low with a bundle, resting or rummaging. Their exact poses and burdens are the kind of detail that can soften or sharpen from one printing to the next, so look at your own sheet and see what your figures are doing. But the headcount of the embrace itself is firm: three at the trunk, arms linked, trying to close a circle they cannot close.

### 6.3 The mountain, demoted

Now go and find Fuji.

You have to go _find_ it, and that hunt is the design working on you exactly as Hokusai intended. On the named sheet the mountain sits to the right of the great trunk, small, a pale and quiet blue, with white clouds drifting across so that part of the cone is veiled. It is unmistakably Fuji, the snow, the perfect slope, but it has been pushed back and shrunk down and half-hidden behind weather, and it holds about as much of your attention as a distant hill. The biggest thing in Japan, parked in the corner behind a tree, peeking out from a cloud.

Here is where this sheet talks directly to the picture we started the book with, so hold the _Great Wave_ in your mind for a moment. There, you will remember, Hokusai built his signature move: a huge near thing and a tiny far thing in the same frame, with the small one as the secret point. The wave loomed enormous in the foreground and Fuji sat far away, no bigger than your thumbnail, and the whole composition swept your eye across the violence and set it down gently on the little eternal mountain.

This sheet is that move turned inside out.

Look at what is the same and what has flipped. As in the Wave, there is a huge near thing and a tiny far Fuji. But in the Wave the near thing was water, formless, momentary, about to collapse, and the picture used all that motion to deliver you, finally, to the still mountain as its quiet reward. Here the near thing is a tree, the most still and rooted thing imaginable, and it does not deliver you anywhere. It just stands there, vast and central, refusing to step aside, and Fuji never gets to be the point at all. In the Wave the small Fuji was the destination. Here the small Fuji is the punchline. Same arithmetic of scale, opposite joke. One picture makes you feel the mountain's serene permanence by setting it against chaos; the other makes you smile at the mountain's sudden smallness by setting it against a single fat tree and three men who care more about the tree.

That is the thing to carry out of this chapter: the composition you learned to read in front of the Wave is not a fixed recipe Hokusai repeats. It is a move he plays in both directions. He can make the far Fuji the gentle goal of your gaze, or he can make it the thing the picture cheerfully overlooks. Once you have seen him do both, you stop reading these prints as portraits of a mountain and start reading them as a designer's decisions about where to send your eye, and how to feel about the place it lands.

#### Definition: Composition, again

In Chapter 1 we defined composition as the arrangement of shapes in the frame and the path that arrangement makes your eye travel. Hokusai, we said, was a deck-stacker: he could not force you to look in an order, but he could stack the design so heavily that you almost always did.

In plain language, this sheet is the proof that the deck can be stacked either way. The arrangement that put Fuji at the end of the journey in the Wave is the same kind of arrangement that buries Fuji behind a tree here. The shapes changed, the destination changed, the feeling changed, but the underlying craft, controlling where the eye goes and in what order, is identical. A designer this good is not painting the same mountain again and again. He is solving the same problem, _where do I want your eye, and what do I want you to feel when it gets there_ , with a different answer every time.

### 6.4 Why a tree, and why this big

It is worth asking why anyone would build a picture this way, and the honest answer is that we should be careful, because the deepest intentions of a print designer two centuries gone are not always recoverable. What we can do is look at what the picture itself makes us feel, and trust that.

What it makes you feel is humility. Not solemn, churchy humility, the lighter kind that comes with a smile. The men are not awed into silence; they are doing something almost playful, hugging a tree to see how big it is, the way you might. The picture lets nature be so large that the only sane human response is to reach out and touch it, to confirm with your own arms that yes, it really is that big. Fuji, meanwhile, the official object of all this region's reverence, gets quietly upstaged by a tree nobody came to worship. There is a gentle democracy of wonder in that: the sacred mountain and the roadside tree are both just enormous, and the small warm creatures on the road will marvel at whichever one is closest.

I will not tell you that Hokusai _intended_ a philosophy of humility, because I cannot read his mind, and this book does not invent. But the feeling is there in the looking, available to anyone who finds the three linked hands first and the demoted mountain second. The order in which you find them is the meaning.

#### Common Misconception

You will see this tree called a cypress in a good many captions, on postcards and in casual write-ups, and they are wrong. It is a Japanese cedar, _sugi_ , a cryptomeria, the tall straight conifer that lines the approaches to old shrines all over Japan and grows to exactly this kind of monumental girth and height. The mistake is easy, both are big evergreen conifers, but it matters, because the _sugi_ is the great sacred-grove tree of the Japanese landscape, the one a traveler on a mountain road in Kai would actually have stopped to embrace. Look at the named sheet: a leafy branch enters at the upper left, the bark runs in those long vertical striations, and the whole growth-habit is the soaring column of a cedar, not the scaly spread of a cypress. When you talk about this print, call the tree a cedar. The caption-writers who say cypress have copied each other's error.

### 6.5 Which impression are you looking at, and what the late pulls lose

There is no famous documented "state" change for this sheet the way there is for Kajikazawa, where the very first pull was a pure all-blue _aizuri-e_ and the later sheets added a red jacket and green rocks. We met that sharpest-of-all impression lessons in Chapter 5. Mishima Pass is quieter. It does not carry a headline before-and-after. But quieter does not mean uniform, and there is still plenty to ask of your particular sheet.

Start with the sky and the hills. The graded wash behind the mountain, the _bokashi_ we learned to read in Chapter 1, the smooth fade from deeper color down to pale, is wiped onto the block by hand for every single sheet, so its depth and its reach differ from impression to impression. Open two Mishima Passes side by side and you may find one with a richer band of blue across the top and one washed paler, one with greener, more saturated hills and one with hills gone soft and grey. Neither is wrong. Each is a separate act of the printer's judgment, a different day's wiping.

Then go to the bark, and here is a piece of looking worth doing carefully. The trunk's vertical striations are carried by the key-block, the carved outline block, and the key-block is the part that wears. On a fresh early pull, taken when the block was crisp, those vertical lines in the bark should read as clean, separate, confident strokes running up the trunk. On a late pull, taken after the block had printed thousands of sheets and the fine ridges of wood had begun to round and break down, you would expect those same striations to look softer, fatter, or broken, with some of the crispness gone out of the grain. So look at your trunk. Are the bark lines sharp and distinct, or have they thickened and blurred? You are not being asked to date the sheet to a year. You are being asked to notice whether you are holding a fresh impression or a tired one, the single habit this whole book is trying to give you, here applied to the one surface, the bark, where the wear shows first.

### 6.6 Looking Prompts

These are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet, not quiz questions. Open a good impression of _Mishima Pass in Kai Province_ before you begin, the Met's JP2970 if you have no other.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Find the three men with their arms around the trunk, hands joined or nearly joined. Stay with them a moment. Then go and find Mount Fuji. How long did the second search take, and what does that delay tell you?

Hint

The men are at the base and middle of the great central trunk, leaning in, arms spread. Fuji is small and pale blue, off to the right of the trunk, partly behind drifting white cloud. You have to look for it.

**Answer:** Almost everyone finds the men and the tree instantly and then has to hunt for the mountain, and that delay is the picture's whole argument. Hokusai has stacked the design so that the trunk and the linked human chain seize your eye first and hold it, while the sacred mountain waits, demoted, in the corner behind a cloud. The gap between the two searches _is_ the meaning: a tree, and the small warm people measuring it with their bodies, have outweighed the most revered mountain in Japan. The companion can use this to show that _order of discovery_ carries feeling. We did the same move in Chapter 1, where finding the crouching boatmen turned a pretty wave into a scene of danger. Here, finding the tiny mountain last turns reverence into a gentle joke.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Notice that the trunk runs straight up and out of the top of the frame while the men, the road, and the distant mountain all lie low and horizontal. What is that vertical doing to your eye, and to the picture's sense of scale?

**Answer:** The trunk is one tall unbroken vertical planted in the dead center of the sheet, and on the named impression its bark is drawn in long vertical striations that make the whole column seem to stream upward. Because it exits the top of the frame, the picture refuses to show you the tree's full height, which is exactly how a too-big thing feels in life: it goes up past where you can take it in. Your eye climbs the trunk, fails to reach the top, and comes back down to the small horizontal humans clinging to it. The men are pinned between that soaring vertical and the low horizon, and the contrast, one great upright against all those low flat shapes, is what makes them feel small. Scale here is not stated, it is built out of the direction of lines.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** This sheet has no famous documented state-change, but your impression still has a particular character. Look at the sky and hills, then at the bark. What varies, and what would tell you whether you are holding a fresh early pull or a worn late one?

**Answer:** Two places to look. First, the _bokashi_ , the graded wash in the sky behind the mountain and the color in the hills: these are wiped by hand on every sheet, so depth and reach differ, and your sheet may carry a richer or a paler band of blue, greener or greyer hills, than someone else's. That is the printer's daily judgment, not a flaw. Second, the bark striations on the trunk, carried by the key-block, which is the part that wears. Crisp, clean, separate vertical strokes in the bark point toward a fresh early pull from a sharp block; softened, thickened, or broken striations point toward a late pull after the block had tired. Unlike Kajikazawa in Chapter 5, there is no dramatic all-blue first state to crown here. The lesson is the subtler one: even a sheet with no headline state is one impression among many, and the bark will quietly tell you which kind you hold.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this beside the _Great Wave_ (Chapter 1) and _Red Fuji_ (Chapter 2). In the Wave, Fuji is tiny and far, the gentle destination your eye is steered toward. Here, Fuji is tiny and far, but it is the thing the picture overlooks. In _Red Fuji_ it is colossal and alone, the only thing in the frame. The same artist, in the same series, makes the same mountain the smallest thing twice and the only thing once. What does that tell you he was actually doing with Fuji?

**Answer:** It tells you he was _using_ the mountain, not portraying it. Three pictures, one peak, three completely different jobs. In the Wave, the huge near wave hands your eye across its chaos and sets it down on the small still Fuji as a reward, the calm at the eye of the storm. In Mishima Pass, the huge near tree refuses to hand you anywhere, and the small Fuji becomes the punchline of a joke about scale, upstaged by a cedar and three men hugging it. In _Red Fuji_ , the storm and the tree and the people are all stripped away and the mountain swells to fill almost the whole sheet, asking whether it can hold a picture alone. The Wave and Mishima Pass run the _same_ composition, a huge near thing and a tiny far Fuji, in opposite directions, one toward reverence, one toward a smile. Once you see that, you stop asking what Hokusai felt about Mount Fuji and start asking what he wanted _you_ to feel in each particular sheet. That shift, from the mountain as subject to the mountain as a tool for steering your eye and your mood, is one of the most useful things this whole volume can hand you.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the failure of the embrace. The three men reach around the trunk and their hands meet, or nearly meet, but they cannot comfortably close the circle, the tree is just too big, and that small shortfall is the entire emotional point of the sheet. Find the place where the fingers strain and notice that the circle does not quite shut. One thing the crowd misses: that this is the _Great Wave's_ composition flipped inside out, the same huge-near, tiny-far-Fuji arithmetic played for a joke instead of for awe, by the same hand, in the same series. And to view a good one now: the Met's JP2970 (search _Hokusai Mishima Pass Kai Province Met_) is open-access and zoomable; the Art Institute of Chicago (24786), Yale, the British Museum, and the Library of Congress all hold strong impressions you can pull up beside it. Whichever you open, ask the two questions this book keeps asking: whose sheet is it, and how fresh, and let the bark striations answer.

### Chapter Summary

We came to this sheet for one idea, scale and human humility, and we found it in a single gesture: three travelers with their arms thrown around a colossal cedar, joining hands to span a trunk too thick to span, using their own bodies as a ruler because nature has grown past the reach of numbers. We honored a correction along the way, the tree is a Japanese cedar, a _sugi_ , not the cypress so many captions claim, and we read its monumental girth and its upward-streaming vertical bark as the source of the picture's sense of size. Most of all, we watched Hokusai take the signature composition we learned in front of the **Great Wave** (Chapter 1), the huge near thing and the tiny far Fuji, and turn it inside out: here the near thing is huge and rooted, the far Fuji is the one demoted and overlooked, and the move that once delivered the eye to a serene mountain now hides that mountain behind a tree as a quiet joke. Set against **Red Fuji** (Chapter 2), where the peak is everything, this sheet completes a lesson, the same artist makes Fuji the smallest thing here and in the Wave and the only thing in _Red Fuji_ , which means he was never painting the mountain so much as using it. And because even a sheet with no headline state is still one impression among many, we learned to read its hand-wiped _bokashi_ sky and, above all, the wear in its bark striations to ask, as ever, _which one are you looking at_. In Chapter 7 we stay in Kai Province and turn to a picture built on a lie: a summer Fuji standing bare and snow-less above a lake, casting a snow-capped winter reflection that does not match the mountain at all, a deliberate, impossible mirror, and the most delicious "what most viewers miss" in the whole series.

* * *

---

## Chapter 7: The Mountain and Its Lie

There are two mountains in this picture, and they do not agree with each other.

You will not notice that at first. Almost nobody does. The sheet looks, at a glance, like one of the calmest things Hokusai ever made: a wide still lake, a village of low roofs along the near shore, a single mountain rising past the water and its own reflection lying upside down beneath it. It is the kind of view a thousand painters have made and called it peace. Your eye accepts it, registers "Fuji and its reflection," and prepares to move on.

Do not move on. Stop at the reflection, and look at it the way you would check a sum you suspected was wrong. Because the mountain standing up out of the lake is a bare, brown, snowless summer peak, with only a few faint streaks of white near the top, the way Fuji actually looks in the warm months when the snow has melted off. And the mountain hanging upside down in the water below it, its mirror image, the thing that by every law of optics must be the same mountain, is snow-capped. Blue and white and crowned with winter. The reflection is wearing a coat the mountain itself has taken off.

That is the whole chapter. Everything else is detail. Hokusai has painted a reflection that does not match the thing it reflects, and he has done it on purpose, and once you have seen it you cannot unsee it, and the picture you thought was about peace turns out to be about something stranger and better.

### 7.1 Find the impression, and find the lie

Search for _Hokusai Reflection Lake Misaka Kai_ , or open the Metropolitan Museum's open-access page and look up accession **JP2969** , which came to the Met in 1939 with the Henry L. Phillips Collection. That is a good sheet to anchor to. Strong impressions also live at Yale, at Harvard, at the Rhode Island School of Design's museum. The image I am describing as I write is the widely shared scan labelled, on Wikimedia Commons, "Fuji reflected in Lake Kawaguchi from Misaka pass." Any of these will do. They are all the same design from the _Thirty-six Views of Mount Fuji_ , published by Eijudō around 1830 to 1832, the same series and shop and moment as the _Great Wave_. (As of 2026-06, these are normally viewable on the museums' open-access pages; prints are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel.)

Open it as large as your screen allows. Now do the one thing that unlocks the picture: zoom in on the real cone above the waterline, and then zoom in on the reflected cone below it, and compare them directly, side against side. Look at the snow.

The upper mountain is the colour of dry earth, ochre and brown, snowless or nearly so. Depending on which sheet you have pulled up you may find a few thin white streaks lingering near its summit, the last patches that have not melted, but the body of the mountain is bare. This is summer Fuji, the real Fuji of the warm season, the mountain as you would actually find it standing at Misaka Pass on a hot day.

The lower mountain, the reflection, is snow-capped. Its whole upper third is white, cool blue-white, the Fuji of winter and of every postcard and every idea you have ever held of the mountain. It is the Fuji of the imagination, the one that is always wearing snow.

Two different mountains, one above the other, and the lower one is supposed to be a mirror of the upper one. It is not. A mirror cannot add snow.

### 7.2 The offset, the second tell

There is a second clue, and it confirms the first is no accident.

Run a straight vertical line down from the summit of the real mountain. By the rules of reflection, the reflected summit should sit directly beneath it, plumb, the way your face sits directly beneath itself when you lean over still water. It does not. The reflection is shifted to the left, off the axis of the real peak, so that the inverted mountain hangs not under its original but a little aside from it.

A true reflection cannot do that either. Water reflects straight down. If Hokusai had wanted an honest mirror he had only to copy the cone and flip it and set it plumb beneath, which is the easiest thing in the world and which any competent designer could do in his sleep. He chose not to. He moved the reflection sideways and he changed its season, two deliberate violations of the same simple law, and he did both quietly, in a picture that wears the face of calm.

This is the moment to name what kind of object you are holding. It is not a landscape. It is a visual koan, a small puzzle dressed as a view, a thing that looks like an answer and is actually a question. Hokusai has built a picture whose surface says _here is a lake with a mountain reflected in it_ and whose content says _look harder, because reflections lie, and so do I, and the lie is more true than the fact would have been._

#### Common Misconception

For two centuries people have called this lake **Lake Suwa** , and you will still see that label here and there. It is wrong, and the correction is worth carrying because it is the kind of small fact that sharpens your eye. The water here is **Lake Kawaguchi** , one of the Fuji Five Lakes that ring the northern foot of the mountain, seen from **Misaka Pass** above its eastern shore. That is what the title says, plainly, in the box on the sheet: _Kōshū Misaka suimen_ (甲州三坂水面), "the water's surface at Misaka in Kai Province." Suwa is a different lake, in a different place. The misnomer is old and stubborn, but the title was never ambiguous, and the village along the near shore is a Kawaguchi village. Knowing where you are standing is the first courtesy a viewer owes a picture.

### 7.3 What the lie is for

So why do it? Why break optics on purpose in a series that, elsewhere, can be scrupulously observed?

Because Hokusai is not painting what the lake reflected on one afternoon. He is painting Fuji twice, and letting the two Fujis say different things. The mountain above is the fact of Fuji: a real geological cone that loses its snow in summer and stands there, brown and ordinary, like any large hill. The mountain below is the idea of Fuji: the eternal snow-crowned sacred peak that lives in every Japanese mind whether or not there is snow on the actual summit that week. And here is the quiet wit of it. He has put the fact up in the air, in the world of light and weather where things change, and he has put the idea down in the water, in the world of reflection and imagination, where Fuji is always itself, always white, always the postcard. The reflection is _truer to the idea of the mountain than the real mountain is to itself._

That inversion is the joke and the wisdom both. We are used to thinking the solid thing is real and the reflection is the illusion. Hokusai turns it over. In his picture the reflection holds the truer Fuji, the one that matters, the one people climb and worship and paint, while the "real" cone above is just this summer's particular bare rock. The image asks you, gently, which mountain you actually believe in: the one your eye reports or the one your mind already knows.

Notice where the snow appears. This is one of those moments where Hokusai's white does its deepest work. All through this book he uses white, the bare un-inked paper or a pale block, to carry his most charged effects: the foam of the _Great Wave_ , the spray of the waterfalls to come. Here the white of snow shows up in exactly the one place it should not, on the reflection rather than the mountain, and that single misplacement is the entire meaning of the picture. The white is doing what white always does for Hokusai, but it is doing it as paradox. Snow where snow cannot be is the whole argument, made in one colour.

### 7.4 The calm that hides the trick

Pull back now and take in the rest of the sheet, because the genius of the deception is that nothing around it raises an alarm. Everything else is perfectly, disarmingly ordinary.

The sky is a deep band of Prussian blue across the top, the same fugitive _bero-ai_ whose moods you have been learning to read, grading smoothly down toward the horizon where it pales almost to nothing. That graded wash is bokashi, the printer's wiped gradient, and it gives the whole upper sheet the still, clarified light of a calm day. Along the near shore sits a small village, a cluster of low houses with thatched and tan-coloured roofs nestled between the foot of the cone and the water. Dark green wooded hills flank the scene on either side, framing the lake. And at the lower right, easy to miss, a single small boat rides the water, a cargo or fishing craft with one lone figure aboard, the only human being in the picture, going about a day's quiet work.

Find that boatman. He is the human measure here, the way the rowers were in the _Great Wave_ and the three travelers were at Mishima Pass in the last chapter. But where Mishima's tree dwarfed three men to teach you scale, this picture gives you one small figure on a calm lake who has no idea he is floating in the middle of an optical impossibility. He sees a mountain and its reflection, the way you almost did. The picture is doing something extraordinary directly above his head and he, like most viewers, is not looking up to check whether the two cones agree.

That is the book's quiet game of what-you-would-miss at its absolute purest. Of all the secrets this book teaches you to find, this is the one most completely hidden in plain sight. The meaning is not tucked into a corner or shrunk to the size of a thumbnail. It is the largest thing in the picture, the mountain itself, painted twice, contradicting itself in full view. And still, the overwhelming majority of people who have ever looked at this sheet have walked away thinking they saw a peaceful lake. The trick works precisely because it is too big and too quiet to suspect. Hokusai folded the whole meaning into the one move nobody bothers to verify.

### 7.5 Which Fuji is wearing snow today?

Now the impression question, which here takes an unusually pretty form.

The amount of snow on each cone, and the depth of the sky's blue, are not fixed across every sheet. They vary by impression and by inking. On some pulls the upper, "real" cone is almost completely bare; on others a printer left a few more white streaks near its summit, so the contrast with the snowy reflection is slightly softer. The sky's Prussian blue runs from deep and saturated on a fresh early pull to paler and greyer on a faded or later one, since _bero-ai_ is fugitive and light eats it over the years. So before you decide how stark the paradox is, look honestly at the particular sheet in front of you.

This matters for the chapter's whole point. The lie is in the design, carved into the blocks, and it holds across every impression: the reflection is always snow-capped while the cone above is always far barer, and the reflection is always offset to the left. That is structural, and you can assert it of any sheet. But _how loud_ the lie speaks, how shocking the mismatch feels, depends on your impression. A crisp early pull with a deep dark sky and a near-bare summer cone makes the snowy reflection leap out almost violently. A softer, faded later sheet mutes the whole effect into something dreamier. Neither is the "real" picture. They are two readings of the same joke, told at different volumes.

So the question to carry to the glass is not only _do the two Fujis disagree_ (they always do) but _how hard does this particular sheet make me work to catch them at it?_ The fresher and bluer your impression, the more the trick wants to be seen.

### 7.6 Looking Prompts

These are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet. Open a good impression first, and be ready to zoom.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Zoom in on the mountain standing above the waterline, and look at how much snow it has. Now zoom in on its reflection in the lake and look at the snow there. What is wrong?

Hint

Compare the two cones for colour, not shape. One is brown and bare or nearly so. The other is capped with white. They are supposed to be the same mountain.

**Answer:** This is the chapter's gift, and the moment to let the learner find it for themselves rather than telling them. The upper cone is a snowless or nearly snowless summer Fuji, brown and ochre, perhaps with a few faint white streaks lingering near the top. The reflection below is snow-capped, blue-white, the idealized winter mountain. A reflection cannot add snow that is not there, so the two cannot both be honest. Hokusai has painted Fuji twice and made the two versions disagree on purpose. The companion's job is to slow the looking down until the learner sees the mismatch with their own eyes, because the discovery is the whole experience. After that, the eye will not let it go, and the calm lake becomes a puzzle.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Start at the summit of the real mountain and let your eye climb up its bare slope. Now let it fall back down, past the waterline, into the reflection. What changes as you cross the surface of the lake?

**Answer:** Going up, you are in the world of fact: brown rock, summer, the mountain as it actually stands at Misaka, with the deep Prussian-blue sky behind it. Crossing the waterline and dropping into the reflection, you pass into the world of the idea: the cone puts on snow, turns blue-white, becomes the eternal sacred Fuji of the imagination. The lake's surface is the border between the two. Notice also, on the way down, that the reflected summit does not sit plumb beneath the real one; it is shifted to the left, a second quiet violation of how reflections work. The eye-path here crosses from observed reality into idealized memory, and the design makes that crossing physical, a literal descent through the water's surface from the true mountain to the truer one.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Look at how much snow your sheet leaves on the upper cone, and how deep its sky-blue runs. Open a second impression if you can. How starkly does each one tell the lie?

**Answer:** The design is fixed: every impression has a snow-capped reflection under a far barer cone, offset to the left. But the volume varies. A fresh early pull with a saturated deep-blue sky and an almost completely bare summer summit makes the contrast leap out, the paradox loud and immediate. A faded later sheet, with greyer blue (the fugitive _bero-ai_ eaten by light) and perhaps a few more white streaks left on the upper cone, softens the mismatch into something gentler and dreamier. Neither is more correct. The companion can use this to reinforce the book's spine: you are always looking at one impression of an edition, and the same joke can be told loudly or quietly depending on which sheet history handed you.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Set this beside a Fuji where Hokusai plays it completely straight, say the bare _Red Fuji_ of Chapter 2, or the calm distant cone behind Mishima Pass in Chapter 6. When does Hokusai tell the truth about the mountain, and when does he lie?

**Answer:** In _Red Fuji_ he gives you the mountain almost without comment, a single honest cone filling the sheet; at Mishima he shrinks Fuji to teach you scale but does not falsify it. Here he deliberately falsifies, painting the mountain twice and letting the two disagree. The comparison reveals that Hokusai's Fuji is never simply reported; it is always _used_ , exactly as we first saw in Chapter 1 where the _Great Wave_ deployed the mountain as the calm at the storm's eye. Sometimes the use is straightforward (be still, be huge, be the measure of these tiny men), and sometimes, as here, the use is a paradox that asks you which mountain you actually believe in, the one your eye reports or the one your mind already knows. The lesson is that there is no neutral Fuji in this series. Every appearance is an argument, and learning to ask _what is he using the mountain to say this time_ is one of the sharpest tools the book can give you.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the two cones, compared directly, the snow on the reflection that the real mountain has shed. Zoom in and check them against each other; this is the single most rewarding act of looking in the whole sheet, and the picture will not perform it for you. One thing the crowd misses: nearly all of it, which is the point. This is the rare masterpiece whose central meaning, a mountain contradicting its own reflection, sits in full view at the largest scale and still goes unseen by almost everyone. To view a good one now: the Met's JP2969 (search _Hokusai Reflection Lake Misaka Kai_), with strong impressions also at Yale, Harvard, and the RISD Museum, all on open-access pages as of 2026-06. Whichever you open, settle first the usual question of whose pull this is and how fresh, and then ask the one that matters more here than anywhere: do the two Fujis agree? They never do.

### Chapter Summary

We came to a picture that looks like peace and found a paradox hiding in plain sight. The mountain above the waterline is a bare summer Fuji; its reflection below is a snow-capped winter Fuji, and the two do not match, while the reflection sits offset to the left rather than plumb beneath the real summit. Neither violation is an error. Hokusai broke the rules of optics on purpose, putting the _fact_ of Fuji up in the changing air and the _idea_ of Fuji down in the still water, so that the reflection holds the truer mountain. We saw the white of snow appear in exactly the one place it should not, and recognized that single misplacement as the whole meaning of the sheet, one more lesson in how Hokusai's white carries his deepest charges. And we met the what-you-would-miss habit at its purest, a meaning so large and so quiet that almost everyone walks past it. Where Chapter 6 used scale (a tree that dwarfs the mountain) to teach humility, this chapter uses contradiction to teach attention. Both belong to the running discovery, opened back in Chapters 1 and 2, that Hokusai never simply paints Fuji; he _uses_ it, sometimes truthfully and sometimes, as here, with a deliberate and meaningful lie. In Chapter 8 we turn the theme inside out. At the Sazai Hall, Hokusai stops hiding the act of looking and paints it directly: a veranda of people, their backs to us, gazing at a tiny distant Fuji, one of them pointing. Having just learned how much we fail to see, we go next to watch other people in the act of seeing.

* * *

---

## Chapter 8: A Crowd That Looks Away

Almost everyone in this picture has turned their back on you.

That is the first strange thing, and it is worth letting it stay strange for a moment before we explain it away. A line of people, about ten of them, stands along a wooden veranda that runs across the upper part of the sheet. They are dressed for a day out, in robes of different colors, and not one of them is looking at us. They are all facing the same way, away, out across a flat open distance toward something on the horizon. We are behind them. We are looking at the backs of their heads and shoulders, at the line of them strung along the rail like birds on a wire, and we are following their gaze out to the thing they have all come to see.

That thing is Mount Fuji. Small, pale, snow-capped, sitting low on the far horizon across the plain. The size of your thumbnail again, exactly as in the _Great Wave_ , the biggest mountain in Japan made tiny by distance. But here something has changed that changes the whole book. In the wave, Fuji was the still point your eye was _steered_ toward by the design. Here Fuji is the point a crowd of painted people are _looking at_ , on purpose, because they climbed up here to do precisely that. You are not just being aimed at the mountain. You are watching other human beings aim themselves at it.

Here is the hinge of the whole volume. This is a book about learning to look, and here, near its middle, Hokusai has given us a picture of a crowd doing exactly that. He has painted _looking itself_.

### 8.1 Find the building, then climb it

Search for _Hokusai Sazai Hall Five Hundred Rakan Met_ , or open the Met's open-access page at metmuseum.org and search the accession number **JP2984** (as of 2026-06, normally viewable on its open-access page; like all these sheets it is light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel). That is the impression I am looking at as I write, in the Henry L. Phillips Collection at the Metropolitan Museum, the same fine 1939 gift that holds so many of these _Thirty-six Views_. Harvard holds a strong impression too, as do the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston, the Art Institute of Chicago, and Princeton. The file I have open is the Met's, served through Wikimedia, catalogued there as MET DP141020. Open one full size and set it beside this page.

The title printed on the sheet is _Gohyaku Rakanji Sazaidō_ (五百らかん寺さざゐどう), which means "the Sazai Hall of the Temple of the Five Hundred Rakan." A _rakan_ (羅漢) is an _arhat_ , an enlightened disciple of the Buddha, and the temple was famous for its set of five hundred sculpted figures of them. The Sazai Hall was a tall structure inside that temple complex in Edo, the city now called Tokyo. It was three stories high, and it had a peculiar nickname. _Sazae_ (栄螺) is the turban-shell, the spiral sea-snail you find in a tide pool, and people called this the turban-shell hall because its staircase coiled up through the building like the inside of that shell. It was built in 1741, and ordinary people climbed it for one reason above all: from the top, on a clear day, you could see Fuji.

Hold onto that, because it is the quiet engine of the whole design. The Sazai Hall was a viewpoint. It was, in the plainest sense, a tourist attraction, a place you paid to climb so you could look at the famous mountain. So the people lined up along this veranda are not allegorical figures or pilgrims in a sacred trance. They are day-trippers. They have come up the spiral stair to see the view, and Hokusai has caught them in the act, from behind, at the moment they all turn to look. The subject of this print is sightseeing. It is one of the most modern subjects imaginable, and he made it almost two hundred years ago.

### 8.2 The line of backs, and the arm that points

Let your eye land on the row of figures, because that is where the design wants you, and walk along it slowly from right to left.

They overlap. That is the honest situation in front of the sheet, and it matters more than it sounds. Where one robed shoulder passes in front of the next, the silhouettes merge, so that if you try to count heads you will get a slightly different number each time. About ten, maybe eleven. I am not going to give you a firm figure and neither should you, because the picture itself does not give one cleanly; the crowd blurs into a crowd, which is what crowds do. Resist the urge to pin it down. The looseness is part of the truth of the thing.

Now find the figure at the far left of the line. This one is wearing green, and this one is doing something the others are not. An arm is raised, and it points, out and away, toward the horizon. Toward Fuji. Follow that arm. It is a line drawn inside the picture by a person inside the picture, and it runs from the pointing hand straight out across the empty plain to the small far cone. _There. There it is._ You can almost hear it. One person in the group has spotted the mountain and is showing it to the others, and in pointing it out to them, the painted figure is pointing it out to you.

This is the second time in this book you have been handed the picture's real action by a single small gesture you might have walked past. In the _Great Wave_ it was the crouching rowers, hidden in plain sight, who turned a pretty curl of water into a matter of life and death. Here it is one raised green arm. Find it, follow it, and the whole sheet snaps into focus as a picture _about_ attention: a group of people, the act of pointing, the thing pointed at, and you at the back of the group, looking along the same line.

#### Common Misconception

It is tempting to read these turned backs as Hokusai being shy of faces, or as a quirk of "Eastern" reticence about the human figure. Neither is true, and the wall-label haze that suggests it should be brushed off. Hokusai could draw a face better than almost anyone alive; the _Manga_ we will reach in Chapter 17 is full of faces caught mid-laugh and mid-yawn. The backs here are a deliberate compositional choice, and a brilliant one. By turning the figures away, he makes them stand-ins for us. We cannot see what they feel on their faces, so the only way to share their experience is to look where they are looking. The turned back is not a withholding. It is an invitation to step into their line of sight.

### 8.3 Everything aims the same way

Now lift your eye off the people and look at how the rest of the design is built, because Hokusai has quietly arranged the entire sheet to do one job: to send your eye to the same far point the figures are watching.

Look down at the veranda floor. The planking runs in long boards toward the distance, and the lines between the boards do not stay parallel the way real planks would. They tilt and converge, narrowing as they recede, so that if you traced them onward they would meet at a point out near the horizon. This is perspective, the European trick of making parallel lines rush together to fake depth, and Hokusai had studied it from imported pictures and bent it to his own ends. The floor is not just a floor. It is a set of arrows. Every seam in the wood is aimed at the distance, dragging your eye along it.

So count the forces now pointing the same direction. The backs of the figures, all facing out. The green arm, pointing out. The planks of the floor, converging out. Three separate systems, the human crowd, the single gesture, the architecture itself, and all three are aimed at one small spot on the horizon where a pale mountain sits. You are not given a choice about where to look. You are pulled, gently and completely, into the crowd's line of sight, until you are looking at exactly what they are looking at. This is composition as directed attention, the signature move we named at the wave, and it has never been more naked than here, because here the thing the design is doing _to_ you is also the thing the picture is _about_.

### 8.4 The eaves, the lantern, and the latticed edge

Spend a moment on the architecture itself, because the looking happens inside a real and particular place and Hokusai has built it with care.

Up in the top right corner, the eaves of the temple roof come down heavy and thatched, a thick dark mass overhanging the scene. From under them hangs a lantern, bell-shaped, suspended in that upper-right corner like a held note. It gives the eye something solid and near to push off from before it travels out to the far-off mountain; the deep near roof and the tiny far cone set a big near thing against a small distant one just as the wave did, only now the near thing is a building and the far thing is the destination of everyone's gaze.

Down the right edge runs a latticed wall, a window or screen of crossed wooden bars. Notice how its tight, regular grid sits against the long converging sweep of the floor planking. The lattice holds the right side of the picture still and orderly while the floor rushes away into depth. Hokusai is balancing a stayed, gridded near-corner against a plunging perspective, and the balance is what keeps the sheet from tipping over into pure recession. Look for the lattice; it is easy to read past, and it is doing real structural work.

#### Definition: Bokashi (again, in the sky)

Now look at the sky above the far plain. The color is deep Prussian blue along the top, and it lightens as it comes down toward the horizon, fading smoothly rather than stopping at a hard line. That graded wash is _bokashi_ , the printer's hand-wiped gradient we met at the wave.

In plain language: to get that smooth fade from deep blue to pale, the printer wiped the ink across the block thick at the top and thin at the bottom, then pressed each sheet fast before it dried, re-wiping for every single pull. The serene blue sky over this little crowd is not Hokusai's design alone. It is the printer's craft, one of the three hands, performed once per sheet, hundreds of times. On a fresh early impression like the Met's, that blue is rich and the fade is clean. On a faded later sheet it goes pale and grey, the way all this _bero-ai_ blue eventually does when light eats it. So when you look at the sky here, you are also looking at the printer's day, and at the age of the particular sheet in front of you.

### 8.5 A day out to see the mountain

Step back now and let the whole thing be what it is: a small, warm human comedy.

Picture the outing. A clear morning in Edo. A group of friends or a family pays to climb the famous turban-shell tower, winding up the spiral stair past the carved arhats, a little out of breath, until they come out onto the high veranda. And there, far off across the plain, is Fuji. Someone in green spots it first and flings out an arm. _Look, look, there it is._ The others crowd to the rail. They have come all this way, up all those stairs, for this exact instant of recognition, the moment a famous view resolves out of the haze into the real small distant mountain. Hokusai has frozen that instant. Not the mountain alone in its grandeur, as in _Red Fuji_ , but the human pleasure of _seeing_ it, the modest ordinary joy of a sightseeing trip paying off.

There is real tenderness in this, and a little comedy too. These are not heroes. The biggest mountain in Japan is reduced to a thumbnail, and the actual subject swelling to fill the foreground is a row of ordinary people's backs. The mountain is the excuse. The looking is the event. Hokusai, who would spend his whole long life obsessed with this one peak, understood perfectly that for most people Fuji was not a private obsession but a day out, a thing you climbed a tower to glimpse and pointed at and felt glad to have seen. He painted that gladness from behind, and let us join the back of the line.

### 8.6 Looking Prompts

These are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet, not quiz questions. Open a good impression of the Sazai Hall, the Met's JP2984 if you can, before you begin.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Walk along the row of figures on the veranda. Now find the one at the far left, in green, and see what its arm is doing. Where is it pointing, and what happens to the whole picture once you have followed it?

Hint

Start at the right end of the line of people and move left until you reach the figure whose arm is raised away from the body. Then run your eye straight out along the direction the arm indicates, across the flat plain, to the horizon.

**Answer:** The leftmost figure, in green, raises an arm and points out across the plain toward the small snow-capped Fuji on the far horizon. The moment you follow that arm, the print stops being a row of people on a balcony and becomes a picture _about_ looking: someone has spotted the mountain and is showing it to the group, and in doing so is showing it to you. This is the same move as finding the rowers in the _Great Wave_ (Chapter 1), where one overlooked detail reorganizes the entire image. The companion can use this to make the learner feel, physically, how a single gesture inside a picture can reach out and direct an eye standing in front of it two hundred years later.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Trace three different things in this picture that are all aimed in the same direction. What are they, and where do they all send you?

**Answer:** First, the backs of the roughly ten figures, all turned to face out across the plain. Second, the single pointing arm of the green figure at the left. Third, the planks of the veranda floor, whose seams converge as they recede, a use of European perspective Hokusai had absorbed and bent to his purpose.
