All three aim at one place: the tiny Fuji on the horizon. The design gives you almost no freedom; the crowd, the gesture, and the architecture together pull your eye into the figures' own line of sight until you are looking at precisely what they look at. This is the book's signature idea, composition as directed attention, in its most self-aware form, because here the thing being done to your eye is also the picture's subject.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Pull up two holdings of the Sazai Hall at once if you can, say the Met's (JP2984) and Harvard's. Look at the sky and the floor. Where do they differ, and which feels earlier?

**Answer:** Certainty is not the goal here; the looking is. Start with the bokashi sky: a deep, clean, richly graded Prussian blue points to an early pull from fresh blocks with newly mixed _bero-ai_ , while a pale, greyed, muddy wash points to a later or light-faded sheet, since this blue is fugitive and the sun eats it over the years. Then check the planking lines and the figures' outlines: sharp and unbroken reads earlier, softened or broken reads later. A fresh sheet and a faded one are not two copies of one experience. They are two different objects with two different lives. Getting the learner to feel that, rather than to win the comparison, is the whole reason to ask.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this beside the _Great Wave_ from Chapter 1. In both, Fuji is tiny and far and is the destination your eye is sent to. What does the crowd do differently in each, and why does it change everything?

**Answer:** In both prints the design steers you across a busy foreground and deposits you on a small distant Fuji, so the mountain is the destination of the eye in each. But look at what the people are doing. In the _Great Wave_ , the boatmen are crouched flat against the boards, fighting for their lives; they have no time to look at the mountain at all, and Fuji's calm is something only we, safe outside the picture, get to notice. Here the people have come precisely to look. Looking is the entire reason they climbed the tower. So the wave makes Fuji a serenity the human figures are too busy surviving to see, while the Sazai Hall makes Fuji the object of a deliberate, shared, leisurely act of attention. Same tiny mountain, same far horizon, opposite human relationship to it: in one, the people cannot look; in the other, looking is all they do. Set the two side by side and you have the whole emotional range of Hokusai's Fuji, from terror to a pleasant afternoon.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the green arm at the far left of the line, pointing. Find it once and the sheet declares itself for good as a picture of people in the act of looking. One thing the crowd misses: the converging floor planks, which most viewers read straight past as mere floor, when in fact they are a set of perspective arrows aiming the eye at the same far point the figures watch. And to view a good one now: the Met (JP2984), Harvard, the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston, the Art Institute of Chicago, and Princeton all hold strong impressions; the Met's, served open-access through Wikimedia as MET DP141020, is the sheet described here. As of 2026-06 these are normally viewable on the museums' open-access pages, though physical sheets are rotated off display to protect them from light, so confirm before you travel. Whichever you open, ask the two questions this book keeps asking: whose impression is it, and how fresh.

And one note for you, the actual person reading this. You have just spent a chapter looking at a picture of people looking. That doubling is not a trick or a curiosity. It is, as plainly as any single sheet can show it, the thing this book asks you to practice. Learning to look is learning to do, deliberately and well, the thing those small painted figures are doing on their veranda: to face the right way, to follow the pointing arm, to find the small far true thing and really see it. Hokusai put a crowd of learners in his picture. You are standing at the back of their line.

### Chapter Summary

We reached the hinge of the book. In Chapter 1 the _Great Wave_ taught us that Fuji is the destination the design sends your eye toward; here Hokusai turns that idea inside out by painting a crowd of about ten sightseers who have climbed the turban-shell tower of the Temple of the Five Hundred Rakan to do, on purpose, the very thing this book is teaching, to look at the mountain. Their turned backs make them stand-ins for us; the single pointing green arm and the converging veranda planking aim our eye, with theirs, at one tiny snow-capped cone on the horizon. We named that as **composition as directed attention** in its most self-aware form, and we read the heavy thatched eaves and hanging lantern, the latticed right edge, and the printer's **bokashi** blue sky as the working of one of the three hands. Where Chapter 7's Misaka reflection turned on _what most viewers miss_ , this sheet turns on _the act of looking itself_. In Chapter 9 we come back down into the city of Edo and meet the Tatekawa timberyard: Fuji glimpsed not from a scenic tower but through a working grid of stacked lumber, where indifferent laborers ignore the mountain entirely, and where the publisher Eijudō has slyly worked his own advertisement into the carved inscriptions on the wood, opening a door onto how these prints were sold.

* * *

---

## Chapter 9: The City as a Grid

Stand back from this sheet and squint, the way you would at a painting you suspect of hiding something, and what you see is not a landscape at all. It is a diagram. A thicket of tall straight lines shoots up the right side like the strings of some enormous instrument. A heavy squared mass climbs the far left into a kind of tower. Across the lower middle, long boards slice down at clean angles, crossing the verticals, ruling the open yard into wedges and triangles. The whole picture is a lattice. Before you have found a single human being in it, before you have even located the mountain it is supposedly about, your eye has been handed a structure of nearly pure line and angle, as severe and as satisfying as a sheet of graph paper.

And then you start finding the people, and the timber turns back into timber, and the diagram turns back into a working day. That double take, from abstraction to labor and back, is the whole pleasure of this print. It is also a door, if you walk through it, into the least romantic and most revealing fact about every sheet in this book: these were a _product_. Somebody paid to make them, owned the means of making them, and sold them for money. And in this one picture, almost as a joke, that somebody has signed his advertisement onto the lumber.

### 9.1 Find a good one, and find your bearings

Search for _Hokusai Tatekawa Honjo Met_ or go to metmuseum.org and search the accession number **JP2982** , the impression in the Henry L. Phillips Collection at the Metropolitan Museum of Art, given in 1939 (as of 2026-06, normally viewable on its open-access page; like all these prints it is light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel). The image I am looking at as I write is the Met's open file on Wikimedia Commons, catalogued there as DP140997, the same sheet at generous width. The Art Institute of Chicago holds a strong impression too (accession 36674), as do the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston and the Los Angeles County Museum of Art. Open one full size and set it beside this page.

The title printed on the sheet is _Honjo Tatekawa_ (本所立川), which names a real and very specific place: the Tatekawa, a canal in the Honjō district on the east bank of the Sumida River, the part of Edo (now Tokyo) given over to the timber trade. Honjō was the city's lumberyard. Wood arrived by water, was stacked to season, and was cut and sold from yards strung along the canal. The name you sometimes see, _Takekawa_ , is an older or variant reading of the same characters; do not let it confuse you, it is the same place. So before you have looked at anything, the title has told you the genre of what you are about to see. Not a beauty spot. A working district. A place where men make a living out of wood.

### 9.2 The grid, and the eye that builds it

Let your eye go back to the design and trace what is actually holding it up.

On the right, a dense cluster of tall thin poles stands straight up, packed close, reaching nearly to the top of the sheet. These are uprights, lumber stood on end to season and to be counted, and Hokusai has drawn them as a near-vertical screen of parallel lines. On the far left, balancing them, a big stack of squared planks and beams is built up into a tower, a solid block of horizontals and verticals, weighty where the poles are light. Between and below these two masses, in the open lower-center of the yard, long boards lie and lean at angles, propped and stacked, cutting clean diagonals across the standing verticals.

That is the engine of the composition: a screen of uprights on the right, a built block on the left, and a scatter of diagonals between them. Verticals, horizontals, diagonals. It is very close to an abstract picture, an arrangement of lines for the pleasure of the arrangement, and it is worth pausing on how unusual that is for a print of the early 1830s. Hokusai is doing here, with stacked lumber, something a European painter would not attempt with such nerve for nearly another century. He has found, in the ordinary geometry of a timberyard, an excuse to build a picture almost entirely out of ruled lines and let the lines themselves be the subject.

#### Definition: Abstraction

Abstraction, in a picture, is the moment when the arrangement of shapes starts to matter as much as, or more than, the things the shapes represent.

In plain language: a realistic picture asks you to look _through_ the marks to the scene, to forget the lines and see the wood. An abstract one asks you to look _at_ the marks, to enjoy the lines as lines, the way you enjoy the pattern in a tiled floor. Most of the _Thirty-six Views_ sit comfortably on the representational side: you look through them to a mountain, a wave, a road. This one keeps tipping you the other way. Every time you settle into reading it as a yard full of wood, the design pulls you back out to the bare grid of it. Hokusai is letting you feel both at once, the lumber and the lattice, and that doubleness is a designer's move as deliberate as the eye-path in the _Great Wave_. There, in Chapter 1, the composition steered you from a huge near thing to a tiny far one. Here it does something colder and more modern: it strips a city block down to its geometry and asks whether geometry alone can carry a picture.

### 9.3 Three men who could not care less about Fuji

Now find the people, because they are the warmth in all this severity, and they reward looking the way the boats in the _Great Wave_ did.

There are three workers, and they are not easy to spot at first because the timber half-swallows them. High on the left, standing on top of the plank-tower, a man is at work with a board, flinging it or holding it up, and if you look just above and beside him you can find a board caught in the air, a single plank in flight, mid-toss. Down and to the right of center, a second man leans over a long beam that is propped across a sawhorse, bent to his task on the angled wood. Lower left, nearer the front, a third figure stands in a robe patterned with blue checks, upright among the boards.

Find all three, and then notice the thing that is the quiet point of the whole picture: not one of them is looking at the mountain. They are looking at the wood. They are working. The lumber is their living and the day is half gone and Fuji, the sacred cone, the supposed subject of this entire celebrated series, is to them simply the thing that happens to be on the horizon every day of their lives, no more remarkable than a lamppost. Hokusai has filled a "View of Mount Fuji" with people who are pointedly _not viewing Mount Fuji_. It is a lovely, sly, human note, and it is sharpest if you have just come from the previous chapter.

In the Sazai Hall of Chapter 8, a crowd climbs a temple veranda and lines up with their backs to us precisely _in order to look at_ Fuji; one of them lifts an arm to point it out, and the whole picture is about the act of gazing. Here Hokusai turns that exactly inside out. Same series, same little mountain on the same horizon, and now nobody looks. The tourists of the Sazai Hall have become the laborers of Honjō, and the mountain that was the whole reason for the earlier crowd's outing is, for these three, beneath notice. Set the two sheets side by side and you have Hokusai's quiet comedy of attention: a city where some people come to look and others are too busy living to bother.

### 9.4 The mountain in the gaps

So where is Fuji? You have to hunt for it, which is itself the joke.

Look into the right background, low on the horizon, and find the small snow-capped cone sitting modestly behind the lumber. It is partly screened by the very poles that dominate the right side of the sheet; the uprights stand between you and the mountain like the bars of a fence, and Fuji shows through the gaps. Between the timber and the mountain, in the middle ground, run the tan and white rooftops of the warehouses where the seasoned wood is stored, and a bright strip of canal water, the Tatekawa itself, the road by which all this lumber arrived. Above it all lies a band of sky in that deep Prussian blue, the _bero-ai_ we met in Chapter 1, fading down toward the horizon.

This is the same compositional habit you have been tracking all through the _Thirty-six Views_ , the huge near thing and the tiny far Fuji, but bent to a new purpose. In the _Great Wave_ the foreground was a wall of water; here it is a wall of worked wood. In both, Fuji is small, far, and easy to miss, the still point past the busy foreground. But here there is an extra twist that belongs to this print alone: the mountain is not merely small, it is _obstructed_. The grid of human industry, the poles and planks of a working city, literally gets in the way of the sacred view. You have to peer between the products of labor to catch the holy mountain at all. That is not an accident of staging. It is the picture's argument, made in pure design: in the real working city, Fuji is something you glimpse between the things you are too busy to stop building.

### 9.5 The publisher signs the lumber

Now we come to the detail that makes this sheet a small masterpiece of the print _business_ , and it is hiding in plain sight on the timber itself.

This is a _supplementary_ print. The _Thirty-six Views of Mount Fuji_ was sold, as its name promises, as a set of thirty-six. It sold so well that the publisher had ten more designs made and added to the series, bringing the real total to forty-six, the over-run we first met in Chapter 1. _Honjo Tatekawa_ is one of those ten extra sheets, made to ride the success of the original set. And the publisher, never one to waste a surface, used the picture to advertise.

Look at the inscriptions written on the lumber, the marks and characters on the planks and poles as if chalked or branded there by the yard. Among them, the publisher Eijudō worked in his own name and an advertisement: words to the effect that this is _the new edition_ , that the _Thirty-six Views_ is now complete. The timber in a timberyard print is carrying a sales notice for the print itself. It is one of the wittiest things in the whole series, and you will walk straight past it unless someone tells you it is there.

#### Definition: Publisher (版元, _hanmoto_)

The publisher, or _hanmoto_ , was the businessman who financed a print, owned the carved blocks, and sold the sheets. He was not the artist and not a craftsman; he was the producer, the one who decided what got made and who paid for it.

In plain language: think of the publisher as the studio behind a film rather than the director or the actors. Hokusai designed; an unnamed carver cut the blocks; an unnamed printer pulled the sheets; but it was Eijudō, the shop run by a man named Nishimuraya Yohachi, who put up the money, owned the physical blocks, and ran the shop where you bought your _Great Wave_ for the price of a bowl of noodles. When the _Thirty-six Views_ sold, it was Eijudō who profited and Eijudō who decided to print ten more. The blocks were his property the way the lumber in this yard is somebody's stock. So when he writes his name and his sales pitch onto the painted timber, he is doing something almost startlingly honest: he is telling you, inside the artwork, exactly who owns it and who is selling it to you.

We met the three hands in Chapter 1, the artist and the carver and the printer, and behind them the publisher. Most of the time the publisher is invisible, a seal in the margin you never decode. Here, for once, he steps into the picture and announces himself. This is the chapter where the _commerce_ of the print, the plain fact that it was a thing financed, owned, and sold, becomes something you can see with your own eyes. A timberyard, after all, is a place where a raw material is stacked, seasoned, and turned into goods for sale. Hokusai has chosen, for the print that advertises the series, a scene that is itself an image of stock-in-trade. The wood waiting to be sold and the print waiting to be sold rhyme with each other, and the publisher's signboard is the hinge between them.

#### Common Misconception

It is tempting to imagine these prints as the private, soulful expressions of a lone genius, the way we think of a Van Gogh canvas. They are nothing of the kind, and this sheet proves it. A _Thirty-six Views_ design was a commercial proposition from the start: the publisher judged that pictures of Fuji would sell, hired Hokusai to design them and craftsmen to make them, and printed as many as the market would buy, then ten more. That commercial origin does not cheapen the art one bit. The pressure to sell is part of what made these images so direct, so legible, so unforgettable. But it does mean that the honest way to look at them includes seeing them as _products of a workshop and a marketplace_ , not relics of a solitary soul. The publisher's advertisement in the lumber is not a blemish on a pure artwork. It is the truth of the thing, written where you can read it.

### 9.6 Looking Prompts

Open a good impression before you begin, the Met's JP2982 or its Wikimedia file DP140997, or the Art Institute's 36674.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Find the three workers among the timber. Start with the man standing on top of the plank-tower at the left, then look for a board caught in the air near him. Then find the man bent over the beam on the sawhorse to the right of center, and the figure in the blue-checked robe at the lower left. Once you have all three, ask: where are any of them looking?

Hint

The lumber half-hides them. Look on top of the left stack for the standing man and the airborne board; lower-right of center for the man leaning over the propped beam; and front-left, lower down, for the checked robe.

**Answer:** None of the three is looking at Fuji, and that is the whole point. They are bent to the wood, working, indifferent to the sacred mountain on their own horizon. Finding them turns the picture from an abstract grid into a working day, and finding _what they ignore_ turns it into a quiet comedy about attention. The man on the stack with the flung board is the one to seek out first; that single plank caught in mid-air is the spark of life in all the ruled geometry, a frozen instant of real labor. The companion can use this to show how Hokusai populates even his most diagram-like designs with bodies actually doing something, and how the act of _not_ looking can be as pointed as looking.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Trace the lines of the design with your finger: the upright poles on the right, the squared tower on the left, the diagonal boards across the middle. Then go looking for Fuji. How long does it take you to find the mountain, and what does that delay tell you?

**Answer:** The grid grabs your eye first; the timber is large, near, high-contrast, and ruled into bold lines, so it dominates completely. Fuji is small, pale, low on the right horizon, and partly hidden behind the very poles you were just admiring. You have to hunt for it through the gaps. That delay is the picture's argument made physical: in a real working city, the mountain is something you glimpse between the things people are busy building. The composition runs on the same logic as the _Great Wave_ of Chapter 1, a looming foreground and a diminished distant Fuji, but here the near thing is human industry, and the far thing is not just small, it is _obstructed_ by the industry. The lattice both frames Fuji and gets in its way.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Set two holdings of this sheet next to each other, say the Met's JP2982 and the Art Institute's 36674. Look at the blue of the sky band, the crispness of the lines ruling the timber, and whether the inscriptions on the lumber read sharp or soft. Which feels earlier?

**Answer:** As always in this book, there is no single right answer, only the habit of asking. This design lives or dies by its lines, so line-crispness matters more here than almost anywhere: a fresh early pull will have clean, sharp, unbroken rules where the poles and boards are drawn, and the tiny written characters on the lumber will be legible. A later or worn sheet softens those edges and may blur the inscriptions, which is a real loss in a print whose whole charge is its ruled geometry and its hidden advertisement. Compare the Prussian blue of the sky too: deep and saturated suggests an earlier or less-faded sheet, washed grey suggests an older one that has lived in the light. The companion's job is not to crown a winner but to make the learner notice that two sheets of the same design are two different physical objects, and that in _this_ design the legibility of the lines is exactly what is at stake.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this beside the Sazai Hall of Chapter 8, where a crowd lines the veranda with their backs to us, gazing out at Fuji, one of them pointing. Same series, same small mountain. What do the people in each picture want from the view, and what does Hokusai do by reversing it?

**Answer:** In the Sazai Hall, the figures exist _in order to look_ ; the picture is about the act of gazing at Fuji, and we, looking at people looking, are pulled into the same attention. Here the three workers are too busy to look at all; Fuji is just the thing on the horizon while they earn a living. Set side by side, the two sheets are a complete little essay on attention: a city has its sightseers and its laborers, and the same sacred mountain is a destination for one and wallpaper for the other. Hokusai, who clearly found the contrast funny, lets the design carry it, the looking crowd versus the working crew. It is also a lesson in how a single series can hold opposite moods, reverence and indifference, without ever changing its nominal subject.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the publisher's advertisement written into the lumber, Eijudō's own name and the notice that the _Thirty-six Views_ is now complete, branded onto the timber of a timberyard. It is the most literal appearance of the print's _commerce_ anywhere in the series, and almost nobody who admires the picture ever notices it. One thing the crowd misses: the small obstructed Fuji on the right horizon, half-screened by the upright poles, easy to overlook entirely in the rush of the grid. And to view a good one now: the Met's open-access JP2982 (search _Hokusai Tatekawa Honjo Met_) and its high-resolution Wikimedia file are excellent and zoomable; the Art Institute of Chicago (36674), the Museum of Fine Arts in Boston, and LACMA all hold strong impressions. Whichever you open, zoom in on the lumber and try to read what is written there, and remember to ask whose sheet it is and how fresh the lines have stayed.

### Chapter Summary

We came to this sheet expecting a view of Mount Fuji and found, first, a near-abstract **grid** , a lattice of upright poles, a squared plank-tower, and diagonal boards ruling a timberyard into pure line and angle, Hokusai the designer pushing his composition almost all the way to abstraction a century before European painters dared. Then we found the **three workers** hidden in the lumber, one flinging a board from the top of the stack, and the quiet human joke that none of them spares a glance for the sacred mountain on their own horizon. That joke landed hardest beside Chapter 8's Sazai Hall, where a whole crowd climbs a veranda _to look_ at Fuji; here, in the working city, nobody bothers, and the little snow-capped cone has to show itself through the gaps in the poles, framed and obstructed by the very industry that ignores it. Most of all, this was the chapter where the **commerce** of the print stepped into the picture: a _supplementary_ sheet, one of the ten extra designs added because the series sold, carrying the publisher Eijudō's own name and sales pitch branded into the lumber. The **three hands** of Chapter 1, and the publisher behind them, became something you could see with your own eyes, written on the wood. In Chapter 10 the format itself changes. We leave the wide landscape sheet for tall vertical ones and step into the waterfall series, where water stops being weather and becomes pure design, falling down the long sheet like a ribbon hung on the wall.

* * *

---

## Chapter 10: Water as a Halo

Turn the sheet. Until now your eye has traveled sideways, the way you read a sentence or scan a horizon, across the wide landscapes of the Thirty-six Views. The wave broke leftward, the wind blew rightward, the timber stacked across the frame. Now the picture stands up. This one is taller than it is wide, a narrow upright sheet you take in from top to bottom rather than side to side, and the change is not a small one. A horizontal picture invites the eye to wander, to stroll along a coast. A vertical picture makes the eye _fall_. And falling, it turns out, is exactly what this picture is about.

So let us look, and let us look the way the design wants us to, from the top down, because there is something waiting at the top that I want you to find before anything else spoils the surprise.

### 10.1 Where to find a good waterfall

Search for _Hokusai Amida Falls Kiso Met_ on Google Arts and Culture, or open the Met's open-access page at metmuseum.org and call up the accession number **JP1085**. That is the impression in front of me, an impression of the print in the Metropolitan Museum of Art (as of 2026-06, normally viewable on its open-access page; these sheets are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel). The Museum of Fine Arts in Boston, the de Young in San Francisco, the RISD Museum in Providence, the British Museum, and the Rijksmuseum all hold this design too. Open one full size and stand it up beside this page.

The full title printed in the box at the upper left is _Kisoji no oku Amida-ga-taki_ (木曽路ノ奥阿弥陀ヶ瀧), which means "the Amida Falls, deep behind the Kiso Road." Keep that word _Amida_ in mind. It is the name of a Buddha, and it is the key to the whole design.

This sheet opens a new series, the first of its kind anyone had attempted: _A Tour of the Waterfalls of the Provinces_ (_Shokoku taki meguri_ 諸国瀧廻り), eight prints, each a single famous fall in a different part of Japan. No one had ever made a print series entirely about waterfalls before. Like the _Thirty-six Views_ , it was published by Eijudō and built in part to show off the new Prussian blue. One thing to keep clear from the start, because the two series sit so close together: this is a _separate, slightly later_ set than the _Thirty-six Views_. The Fuji views came out around 1830 to 1832; this waterfall tour is a different series of its own, dated a few years on. The exact year is a little contested, and I will be honest about that in a moment, but it sits around 1833 to 1834. So when you see both date-ranges in this book, they belong to two distinct series, not one series contradicting itself.

### 10.2 The round head at the top

Let your eye go to the very top of the sheet. There, high on the rock, you will find a large pool of water shaped almost like a circle, a near-round basin sitting up at the head of the fall. And it is filled, this round pool, with swirling lines of blue and white, turned in tight concentric loops like the grain of polished marble or the rings of a cut tree. The water does not lie flat in it. It coils.

Now hold that circle in your eye and say the title to yourself again. _Amida._ Amida is the Buddha of the Western Paradise, and in the painting and sculpture of Japan he is shown, as buddhas are, with a great round halo of light behind his head. Look back at the pool. A perfect glowing circle, high up, radiating concentric rings. Hokusai has made the head of the waterfall into the haloed head of a Buddha. The round basin is a halo; the water inside it is the light. He has taken a thing of pure rushing physics, a pool at the top of a cliff, and quietly rhymed it with the most sacred image he could reach for, and he has done it without a single word of explanation. The place-name said _Amida_ , and he answered the place-name in the shape of the water.

This is one of his conspiratorial jokes, except that _joke_ is the wrong word for something this reverent. Call it a visual pun that means it. Most people who glance at this print see a pretty round pool and move on. The round pool is a god's halo, and the title told you so, and almost nobody reads the title.

### 10.3 Follow it down

Now let the eye do what the vertical format built it to do. Fall.

From the underside of that round haloed head, a single broad column of white water pours straight down the center of the sheet. At the top, just below the pool, it is wide and solid, a clean white band. As it descends it narrows, and near the bottom it frays apart into a handful of fine separate threads, like a thick rope unwinding into strands. Your eye drops with it, top to bottom, the whole height of the picture, from the sacred circle to the splash below. This is the trip the upright sheet was made for. In the _Great Wave_ the eye was flung sideways off a curl. Here it is dropped, vertically, the length of the fall, and the picture is essentially one long plunge with a halo at the top of it.

And the white of that plunging column, here is the thing to notice, is not white paint. It is the bare paper again, the untouched sheet showing through, exactly as the foam of the _Great Wave_ was bare paper. The printer inked the dark rock and the blue water around the fall and left the shape of the falling column unprinted, so that the brightest, most rushing thing in the picture is nothing at all, the blank sheet doing the work of pouring water. Back at the _Great Wave_ in Chapter 1 I asked you to see that the foam was bare paper. Here is the same trick on a grander scale, a whole cataract made of blank sheet.

But look harder at _how_ the water is drawn, because this is where one of Hokusai's lifelong obsessions takes a strange and beautiful turn.

### 10.4 Water turned into pattern

Look again at the round pool, then at the descending column, then at the splash where it lands. All of it, every drop, is drawn as _line_. The concentric rings of the halo-pool, the long parallel filaments of the falling column, the curling swirls where the water hits bottom: this is water stylized into a system of lines, marbled, looping, almost like the pattern on a length of decorative paper or a printed fabric. Hokusai has stopped _describing_ the water and started _designing_ it. The fall is closer to ornament than to observation.

And yet, and this is the marvel, it still reads as falling water. You are not for one second confused about what you are looking at. The looping concentric grain of the pool reads instantly as a churning eddy; the straight parallel threads read as a sheet of water dropping under its own weight; the swirls at the base read as spray and turbulence. He has pushed the living line he uses for everything almost all the way into pure pattern, decorative, repeating, abstract, and stopped at the exact point where it would tip over into meaning nothing. It is water that has become ornament and is still water.

This is the deepest thing the line can do, and it is worth pausing on. In the _Great Wave_ the line described motion, the taut edge of a real wave. Here the same calligraphic line abstracts motion into a pattern of repeating curves, and the pattern is so well chosen that it carries the sensation of flow even though no real water ever looked quite this neat. A real waterfall is a chaos of broken light. Hokusai gives you an orderly diagram of swirls and threads, and your eye accepts it as wetter and more rushing than a photograph would be. He has found the _idea_ of falling water and drawn that.

This sheet is not an _aizuri-e_ , the all-blue print we met in Chapter 5, but it is steeped in the same new pigment, the Prussian blue called _bero-ai_. The waterfall series leans on that blue the way the _Thirty-six Views_ leaned on it, water, rock, and sky all tuned to its key. How much of it survives in any given sheet, though, depends on which impression you hold, and that is the next thing to check.

### 10.5 The travelers, and the scale

Before we leave the water, find the people, because Hokusai has hidden the human measure here exactly as he hides it everywhere.

Low on the left, on a ledge of rock partway down the cliff, a few tiny travelers have stopped to picnic. They are small, almost lost against the dark stone, dwarfed by the wall of water dropping past them. Find them and the whole picture snaps to scale: that round pool at the top, which read as an abstract decorative circle a moment ago, is suddenly understood to be enormous, a basin the size of a courtyard, hanging high above these specks of human beings who have climbed all this way "deep behind the Kiso Road" to sit and look at it. The fall towers. The people are pennies. And they are doing the most ordinary thing in the world, having lunch, in front of a halo of water that Hokusai has quietly made holy.

This is his recurring move, the one we first met under the wave: the tiny human caught against something vast and indifferent. There the men crouched under tons of water about to fall. Here they sit comfortably _beside_ the water, tourists at a sight, and the contrast is gentler, almost affectionate. But the scale lesson is the same. Find the small figures and the large thing becomes large.

### 10.6 Did Hokusai ever see this fall?

Now an honest note, because it changes how you hold the picture.

The Amida Falls were remote, deep in the mountains off the old Kiso highway, the sort of place a famous Edo print-designer in his seventies was unlikely to have hiked to. The strong likelihood, and scholars say this plainly, is that Hokusai never stood in front of this waterfall at all. He worked from travelers' descriptions, from what people who had been there told him it looked like, and from his own formidable imagination. So the fall on your screen is partly designed rather than recorded, a waterfall reconstructed from hearsay and then improved into something more perfectly itself than any real cascade.

This is not a flaw to apologize for. It is, if anything, the point. A man who has actually drawn water ten thousand times does not need to see _this_ water to draw it truly; he needs only to know what falling water _does_ , and then he can build the ideal one. The halo-pool, the narrowing column, the marbled swirls: these are not a portrait of the Amida Falls. They are Hokusai's theory of what a waterfall _is_ , dressed in the name of a real place. Knowing he probably never saw it does not make the picture less true. It makes the design more visibly his.

### 10.7 Which year, and which blue?

Two honest uncertainties, and both are worth carrying.

The first is the date. The Met's own label for this impression reads "ca. 1827." That is an outlier. The mainstream dating of the whole waterfall series puts it around 1833 to 1834, with the rest of Hokusai's great landscape output. I am not going to hand you a single confident year, because the honest situation is a small disagreement: one major catalogue says earlier, most of the field says a few years later. When you look at this sheet, you are looking at a print made in the first half of the 1830s, and the exact year is genuinely unsettled. That kind of modest uncertainty is normal in this world, and pretending it away would teach you a worse habit than living with it.

The second is the blue, and this one you can investigate with your own eyes. The saturation of the Prussian blue in the round pool and the surrounding water varies from impression to impression. Some sheets show a deep, drenched, almost electric blue in that halo; others show a paler, washed-out, greyer blue. Pull up two collections' versions side by side and compare the pool. Is yours a saturated blue that makes the halo glow, or a soft, faded one? Remember from the wave that Prussian blue is fugitive, that light eats it over the decades, and that the printer re-mixed and re-wiped his inks for every single pull. So a pale halo may be a faded sheet that once glowed, or simply a more lightly inked pull. You will not always be able to tell which. But noticing the difference at all is the whole discipline of this book.

### 10.8 Looking Prompts

These are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet. Open a good impression of JP1085, full size, before you begin.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Go to the very top of the sheet and find the large round pool. Look at how the water inside it is drawn. Then say the title, "Amida," to yourself, and ask what that round shape is rhyming with.

Hint

Amida is a Buddha. Think about how buddhas are shown in painting and sculpture, what is always drawn behind the head.

**Answer:** The round pool, filled with concentric blue-and-white swirls, is shaped and placed exactly like the halo behind a Buddha's head, and the Buddha named in the title is Amida. Hokusai has made the head of the waterfall into a haloed Buddha-head, answering the place-name with the form of the water. This is the discovery most viewers walk straight past; they see a pretty circular eddy and never connect it to the word printed in the box. The companion can use this to show how Hokusai folds meaning into a place-name and into a shape at once, the way he hid the boats in the wave. Once the halo has surfaced for you, it stays surfaced, and the whole picture turns from a landscape into something quietly devotional.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** This is a tall sheet, not a wide one. Start at the top and let your eye fall. Where does it begin, where does it end, and what is the design doing to make it drop rather than wander?

**Answer:** The eye begins at the round halo-pool high at the top, then is pulled straight down the single broad white column of falling water as it narrows into threads, and lands at the splash near the bottom, with the tiny picnickers off to the lower left for scale. The vertical format is doing the work: where the _Great Wave_ 's horizontal sheet flung the eye sideways off a curl, this upright sheet drops the eye the full height of the fall. Notice that the brightest, most active shape, the plunging column, is the bare unprinted paper, just as the wave's foam was. The whole design is one long controlled fall from a sacred circle to a human ledge, and the change of format is what makes that fall possible. This is the same designer's command of the eye we met in Chapter 1, turned ninety degrees.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Bring up two holdings of the Amida Falls together and look hard at the round pool at the top. How blue is it in each? Which one glows, which one has gone soft?

**Answer:** There is no single right answer, and that is the lesson. The Prussian blue of the halo-pool varies from sheet to sheet: some impressions show a deep, saturated, almost electric blue that makes the round head blaze; others show a paler, greyer, washed blue. A drenched, vivid pool suggests either a richly inked pull or a sheet that has been kept in the dark; a soft, faded pool suggests a lightly inked pull or, more often, a sheet that has spent decades in the light, since _bero-ai_ is fugitive and light eats it. You usually cannot say for certain which cause is at work, and you do not need to. The point is to see that the two pools are not the same blue, that you are holding one physical object out of many, and that its particular blue is part of its biography. A viewer who can see that difference will never again take a print's color for granted.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this beside the _Great Wave_ from Chapter 1. Both are pictures of water by the same hand. What does each one do with water, and how opposite are the two solutions?

**Answer:** In the _Great Wave_ , water is violence and motion: a real wave caught at the instant before it crashes, its foam-claws reaching down at the boats, the whole picture a frozen split-second of danger. In the Amida Falls, water is stilled into pure pattern: the same falling, churning element drawn as calm marbled rings and orderly parallel threads, a sacred halo and a tidy plunge, with tourists picnicking peacefully beside it. The wave is a moment; the fall is forever, pouring the same way it has always poured. One uses the living line to seize a violent instant, the other uses the same line to abstract endless motion into ornament. That the same artist could make water mean catastrophe in one sheet and serene decoration in another tells you, again, that he was not drawing water. He was using it. Hold this contrast as we go, because the next chapter offers a second, opposite solution to the very same problem.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the halo-pool at the top, and the moment you realize the round head of water is the Buddha's haloed head named in the title. One thing the crowd misses: the tiny picnickers on the ledge at the lower left, who give the whole towering fall its scale. And to view a good one now: search _Hokusai Amida Falls Kiso Met_ or the accession **JP1085** on the Met's open-access pages; the MFA Boston, the de Young, the RISD Museum, the British Museum, and the Rijksmuseum all publish strong impressions of this design (as of 2026-06). Whichever you open, check the blue of that round pool, and ask whose sheet it is and how fresh.

### Chapter Summary

We turned the sheet upright and learned that the change of format changes the looking: a tall vertical picture makes the eye fall instead of travel, and Hokusai built this one as a single long plunge from a sacred circle at the top to a splash and a picnic at the bottom. We found the round halo-pool and read it as the haloed head of Amida Buddha, a meaning folded silently into the place-name. We watched Hokusai push the **living line** of Chapter 1 almost all the way into pure pattern, water stylized into marbled rings and parallel threads, ornament that is still unmistakably water, and we met again the **bare paper** doing the work of the pouring column, exactly as it did the foam of the wave. We were honest that Hokusai likely never saw this remote fall and designed it from travelers' accounts, that the date is genuinely unsettled between the Met's "ca. 1827" and the field's c.1833–34, and that the **blue** of the pool fades and varies from sheet to sheet, so the question _which impression are you holding_ applies here as sharply as it did to the wave. In Chapter 11 we stay in the same waterfall series and meet the Kirifuri Falls, where the water solves the same problem a completely different way: instead of a single column dropping from a halo, the white threads fork and branch down the dark rock like the roots of a tree. Two waterfalls, two answers to the one question Hokusai never tired of asking, how do you draw moving water on a still sheet of paper.

* * *

---

## Chapter 11: Water That Branches Like Roots

Take in the whole sheet of white water at once, the way you would first see a real cascade from across a valley: a pale, spreading mass pouring down a face of dark rock. Now let that impression resolve into its parts. Near the top, where the water first pours over the lip of dark stone, it enters as just a few narrow strands, clean strokes of white against the blue-green. Then, partway down, they begin to split. One strand becomes two. The two run on, and split again, and the splitting keeps happening all the way to the bottom, so that what entered the picture at the top as a handful of streams arrives at the foot of the rock as a whole spreading fan of white veins, fanned out wide, like the roots of an upturned tree or the branching of a river seen from the air. That spreading is what the whole picture is built to show you. Hold onto it. We are going to spend the chapter inside it.

This is _Kirifuri Falls at Mount Kurokami in Shimotsuke Province_ (下野黒髪山きりふりの滝, _Shimotsuke Kurokami-yama Kirifuri no taki_), from the series _A Tour of the Waterfalls of the Provinces_ (諸国瀧廻り, _Shokoku taki meguri_). It is the same series as the Amida Falls you met in the last chapter, the same publisher, Eijudō, the same moment around 1833 to 1834, and the same tall vertical format. But Hokusai has done something almost opposite here, and the difference between the two sheets is one of the most useful comparisons in this whole book. Get a good one in front of you first.

### 11.1 Where to find a good Kirifuri

Search for _Hokusai Kirifuri Falls Kurokami Met_ on Google Arts and Culture, or open the Metropolitan Museum of Art's open-access page at metmuseum.org and search the accession number **JP2924**. That is the impression I am looking at as I write this, a sheet in the Met that came in through the Henry L. Phillips Bequest in 1939 (as of 2026-06, normally viewable on its open-access page; ukiyo-e prints are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel). On Wikimedia Commons the same scan files under MET DP141247. The Art Institute of Chicago holds a strong impression, as do the Philadelphia Museum of Art, the Portland Art Museum, the Los Angeles County Museum of Art, and the Library of Congress, which publishes large scans you can fall into. Open one of these full size and set it beside this page.

The series title in the cartouche at the upper left tells you Hokusai is touring the famous falls of the provinces, the way the _Thirty-six Views_ toured the famous views of Fuji. This one is in Shimotsuke, the old province north of Edo that is now Tochigi, near the temple-town of Nikkō. And the name carries a small gift, which we will come to.

### 11.2 What the water is doing

Now stand back from your single thread and take in the whole field of white.

In most pictures of a waterfall, even most of Hokusai's own, the water comes down as a sheet or a column, one mass of falling white. The Amida Falls in the last chapter did exactly that: a single broad band of water plunging from the round basin at the top, narrowing as it fell. Here Hokusai refuses the column. The water does not fall as one thing. It falls as _many_ things, a whole population of slender white streams that braid past one another and fork apart as they descend, never gathering into a single sheet, splaying wider and wider toward the base. The closest thing in nature is not really a waterfall at all. It is the root system of a tree pulled out of the earth, or the veins on the back of a hand, or a delta seen from a great height. The water has been drawn as a branching structure.

This is doing two contradictory things at once and getting away with both. The pattern is nearly a diagram: the white threads are so deliberate, so evenly drawn, so clearly _designed_ , that you could almost mistake the falls for an abstract motif hanging on a rock. And yet you never for one second doubt that you are looking at water. The threads go the way water goes. They follow gravity, catch on the outcrops, pool and churn at the bottom. Hokusai has pushed the image right up to the edge of abstraction and stopped exactly there, at the last point where pattern and water are still the same thing. That balance is the picture's whole achievement, and it is rarer than it looks.

#### Definition: Stylization

To stylize is to simplify and regularize what you see into a repeatable visual pattern, keeping enough of the real behavior that the eye still reads the thing, while gaining the clarity and rhythm of design.

In plain language: a photograph of this falls would show you a chaos of spray, a thousand random droplets and shifting glints with no shape you could hold in your mind. Hokusai throws nearly all of that away. He keeps only the underlying logic, the way falling water divides and spreads, and he draws that logic as a clean system of branching lines. What you lose is the wet, glittering accident of a real cascade. What you gain is something a camera can never give you: the _structure_ of the falling, made visible, so that you understand at a glance how this particular water behaves. Stylization is not failing to be realistic. It is choosing to show the rule instead of the noise.

### 11.3 The living line as the star

In Chapter 1 we named the first of Hokusai's signature moves, the living line, that taut calligraphic outline cut into the block, alive at every point. There the line served the wave, describing the claw of foam and the edge of the swell while you admired it without quite isolating it. Here the line steps forward and becomes the subject. There is almost nothing in this picture _but_ line. The falls are line; the rock is line. The whole sheet is an argument that a moving, branching system of drawn strokes can carry an entire image on its own.

Follow one thread again and notice how the line behaves. Near the top it is firm and fairly straight, a confident downward stroke. As it descends and forks, the strokes thin and curve, bending around the obstacles in the rock, sometimes whipping into a little hook of spray. The white you are following is the bare paper, exactly as the foam was in the _Great Wave_. The printer inked the dark rock around these threads and left the threads untouched, so the whitest, brightest thing in the picture, the rushing water, is nothing at all, the blank sheet showing through. Every place where a thread forks, an unnamed carver had to cut two clean ridges of cherry wood where there had been one, holding the white channel open between them without a slip. The crispness of that forking, each split staying sharp and separate rather than blurring into a grey smear, is as much the carver's triumph as the designer's. Find an impression where the threads are knife-clean all the way down and you are seeing a carver at the top of his craft and a printer who registered the block to a hair.

Here is the heart of what this chapter has to teach. Hokusai has taken the single hardest thing to draw, moving water, and solved it here not by describing the surface but by diagramming the motion. He draws the _system_. And once you have seen him do it this way, you will never again think of his line as mere outline. It is a tool for showing how things move.

### 11.4 The rock, the foam, and the figures

The water is the star, but the sheet is full, and the rest rewards a slow look.

The rock behind and around the threads is a deep, cool blue-green, dark enough to throw the white water forward and make it glow. Set between the white threads, dotted across the face of the cliff, are craggy little outcrops in a warm ochre-yellow, knobs and ledges of stone that interrupt the falling water and catch the threads as they come down. That warm ochre against the cool blue-green is the only real color contrast in the picture, and Hokusai uses it sparingly, like seasoning. Let your eye hop from one ochre knob to the next and you will find they help organize the whole descent, giving the eye footholds on the way down the cliff.

At the very bottom, where all those branching threads finally arrive, the water gathers into a churning white basin. Here the orderly diagram dissolves into something rougher, heavy curds of foam piling and turning, the one place in the picture where Hokusai lets the water look genuinely turbulent rather than patterned. The contrast is deliberate. The neat forking threads above, the wild foaming pool below: the picture shows you water as design at the top and water as chaos at the bottom, and the journey between them is the descent your eye has just made.

Now look for the people, because they are easy to miss and they change the scale of everything. Down at the lower left and center, near that foaming basin, there is a knot of tiny figures. Some seem to stand and look up at the falls, heads tilted back, the way you would. Higher up, off to the right on the slope, a few more small figures appear, some perhaps climbing the path. Let your eye find them and then guess at what they are doing; the picture is not precise about it, and that vagueness is part of the pleasure. What matters is the jolt of scale. The instant you register that those specks are human beings, the falls leap up to their true enormous size: a cascade you had read as flat pattern becomes a real cliff of plunging water, dozens of times the height of a person, the tiny upturned faces at its foot doing exactly what you are doing.

This is a move you have met before. In the _Great Wave_ the hidden rowers turned a pretty shape into danger and scale; at the Sazai Hall the figures stood with their backs to us and showed us how to look. Here the specks at the foot of the falls do something gentler and just as pointed. They are the picture's built-in viewers, standing where you stand, and finding them is half the chapter's reward.

### 11.5 The mist in the name

Look once more at the whole sheet and then read the place-name again, because Hokusai's titles often fold a meaning into a word, and this one does.

_Kirifuri_ (きりふり) means, quite literally, "falling mist." The falls were named for the fine spray that rises off them as the water shatters on the rocks, a haze of mist that hangs in the air, so that the cascade seems to be falling as mist as much as water. Once you know that, the picture reads differently. All those slender, branching, nearly weightless threads are not just water descending; they are water in the act of becoming mist, breaking apart into ever-finer streams the further they fall, on their way to dissolving into spray. The branching structure and the name are saying the same thing. This is water caught at the moment it stops being a solid sheet and starts becoming air.

And the mountain has a name too. Mount Kurokami (黒髪山) means "black hair" mountain, a peak near Nikkō. It is a quiet rhyme with the picture, if you want one: black-hair mountain, and the white water streaming down its dark stone face like pale strands combed loose. Hokusai does not insist on the pun, and you should not either. But the title is doing more work than a label, as his titles usually are.

### 11.6 Two designs for one substance

Set this sheet beside the Amida Falls from Chapter 10, the two of them open on your screen at once, and you have one of the most instructive pairs in the series.

They are twins by every external measure. Same artist, same series, same publisher, same year, same tall vertical format, same problem to solve: how do you draw falling water on a flat sheet of paper? And Hokusai gives two opposite answers. At the Amida Falls the water is _round and woven_ : it begins as a near-circular basin at the top, a calm pool shaped like a halo, and falls from it as a single coherent column, gathered and curling inward on itself. Here at Kirifuri the water is _forking and splayed_ : it begins as a few narrow streams and immediately divides, throwing itself outward and apart, branching wider the further it goes.

A column that pulls together, against a root-system that flies apart. Both are pure design, both unmistakably water, and they are nearly mirror images. This tells you something no single sheet can. Hokusai was not trying to record what a particular waterfall looked like; he was inventing visual systems for how water _behaves_ , and he could invent more than one for the same substance. Seeing the two side by side is like hearing a great improviser take one melody and voice it two opposite ways at the same keyboard, just to show you the reach of his hand.

### 11.7 The blue, and which impression you hold

A word about the color, which is half the pleasure of this vertical series and the place where your particular sheet matters most.

The dark cliff is built largely on Prussian blue, that intense imported _bero-ai_ , the pigment that made the whole waterfall series possible. Mixed and modulated toward green for the rock, it gives that deep cool ground the water needs to glow against. But Prussian blue is fugitive, and these vertical falls vary a great deal from impression to impression, so I will not tell you exactly what shade your rock is. Look instead, and ask. If your impression is a fresh, early pull, the rock should read as a deep, saturated blue-green, almost cold, and the contrast with the white threads should be stark and electric, every fork knife-sharp. If your sheet is later or has spent years in the light, the blue may have drifted toward a softer grey-green, the threads less crisp where the blocks have worn, the whole picture quieter. Neither is wrong. A faded Kirifuri is not a failed one; it is an older one, a sheet that has lived a life, as we said of the wave.

So judge the rock's depth, the crispness of the forking threads low down where the branching is most intricate and a worn block shows it first, and whether the ochre outcrops still read warm or have gone muddy. Then you will know roughly which sheet you hold, and you will be reading the print as it deserves, as one physical object among many, not a fixed image.

#### Common Misconception

Many people, seeing how regular and designed these threads are, assume Hokusai must have been working from his imagination, that no real waterfall looks so neat. The truth is the other way round. Kirifuri is a real cascade near Nikkō, and it really does come down in many separate braided streams rather than a single sheet, because the rock face breaks the water apart. Hokusai did not invent the branching. He observed it, then clarified it. What looks like pure pattern is a heightened, organized version of how this particular falls actually behaves. That is the discipline behind the stylization: he earns the right to simplify by first seeing exactly what is there.

### 11.8 Looking Prompts

These are not quiz questions. They are openings for a conversation in front of the actual sheet. Open a good impression before you begin.

#### Look closer

**Q1.** Put your eye on one white thread near the top of the falls and trace it slowly downward. Count how many times it splits before it reaches the foaming pool at the bottom. What does the act of following a single strand teach you that taking in the whole white field at once does not?

Hint

Start high, where the threads are fewest and straightest. Watch for the points where one strand becomes two; they happen again and again, and they cluster around the ochre rock-knobs.

**Answer:** The exact count will differ depending on which thread you pick and which impression you hold, and that is fine; the point is not the number. Following one strand turns the picture from a flat decorative pattern into a _process_ unfolding in front of you. You feel the water dividing, again and again, each fork sending the streams a little wider, until the orderly threads arrive at the wild churn below. Taking in the whole field at once, you see a beautiful design. Tracing one thread, you see how the design _works_ , and you catch Hokusai's real subject, which is not the look of the falls but the logic of how falling water spreads. The companion can use this to show that close looking is often a matter of following one thing all the way, rather than glancing at everything.

#### Follow your eye

**Q1.** Notice the splitting of the water as a single visual event, from the few narrow streams at the top to the wide fanned-out roots at the bottom. Which direction does the design make your eye travel, and what happens to your sense of the water as you go?

**Answer:** The branching almost forces the eye downward and outward. You enter at the top, where the water is gathered into a handful of strands, and the forking pulls you down the cliff and spreads you wider with every split, until you arrive at the foaming basin where everything dissolves into turbulence. Going down, your sense of the water changes from order to chaos: neat diagram at the top, wild foam at the bottom. Hokusai has built the descent into the composition, so that simply letting your eye fall is to experience the water falling. That is composition as steering, the move we first named at the wave, here turned vertical and made to enact the very thing it depicts.

#### Which impression are you looking at?

**Q1.** Open two collections' Kirifuri side by side if you can, say the Met's JP2924 and the Art Institute of Chicago's. Look hard at the depth of the blue-green rock and the crispness of the forking threads low down near the pool. Which one feels earlier?

**Answer:** You will not get a certain answer, and chasing one misses the point. Look at saturation, and at the fine forks. A deep, cold, vivid blue-green rock with threads that split knife-clean all the way down means an early pull, struck when the blocks were fresh and the Prussian blue newly mixed. A softer, greyer rock, with threads gone fuzzy or run together where the lower forking turns intricate, means a later or more faded sheet. Look first at the dense branching near the basin, because worn blocks surrender their crowded fine detail there before anywhere else. The comparison is not there to rank the two sheets. It is there to let the learner feel that a print is a physical thing that ages, not a fixed image.

#### Compare

**Q1.** Hold this beside the Amida Falls from Chapter 10. Same artist, same series, same year, same vertical format, same subject of falling water. How does each picture design the water differently, and what does the pair tell you about Hokusai that neither sheet alone could?

**Answer:** The Amida Falls draws water as _round and gathered_ : a halo-like basin at the top, then a single coherent column pulling together and curling inward. Kirifuri draws water as _forking and splayed_ : a few streams that immediately divide and fly apart, branching wider like roots, never gathering into one sheet. They are nearly mirror opposites, both pure pattern, both undeniably water. Together they prove that Hokusai was not copying what a given waterfall looked like; he was inventing visual systems for how water _behaves_ , and he had more than one for the same substance. Seeing the pair is like watching a designer hand you two opposite blueprints for one substance that will not hold a shape, each one complete, each one true.

#### The viewer's note

One thing to seek out: the tiny figures, both the cluster at the foot of the falls near the foaming pool, where some seem to gaze up, and the few small figures higher on the slope to the right, where some seem to climb. Finding them snaps the whole cascade up to its true enormous scale and reveals the picture's built-in viewers, standing where you stand. One thing the crowd misses: the meaning folded into the name. _Kirifuri_ means "falling mist," for the spray that rises off the falls, so all that branching into ever-finer threads is water on its way to becoming air; and Mount Kurokami means "black hair," dark stone with pale water combed down it. To view a good one now: search _Hokusai Kirifuri Falls Kurokami Met_ or the accession **JP2924** ; the Met, the Art Institute of Chicago, the Philadelphia Museum of Art, the Portland Art Museum, LACMA, and the Library of Congress all publish strong, zoomable, open-access impressions (as of 2026-06). Whichever you open, keep the old habit alive: name whose sheet you are holding, and judge how fresh its blue and its forks have stayed.

### Chapter Summary

We followed a single white thread down a dark cliff and watched it fork, again and again, into a spreading root-system of pale streams, and in doing so we caught Hokusai pushing a picture right to the edge of abstraction without ever letting it stop being water. We named that act **stylization** , the choice to show the rule instead of the noise. We watched the **living line** from Chapter 1 step out of the background and become the star, carrying nearly the whole image on its own, the white threads once again the **bare paper** showing through. We read the **figures** that set the scale and the **name** that folds "falling mist" into the very structure of the water. And we set this sheet beside the Amida Falls of Chapter 10 and found two opposite designs for one substance, round and gathered against forking and splayed, proof that Hokusai invented systems for how water moves rather than copying how it looked. In Chapter 12 we stay with the waterfall series one last time and meet the Horse-washing Falls at Yoshino, where the water comes down not as a column or a fan of threads but as a broad stepped cascade, and where a legend, the warrior Yoshitsune washing his horse, is folded into the place-name itself, just as "falling mist" was folded into this one.

* * *

---

## Chapter 12: Water and Legend

A horse is being washed. That is the first thing to fix in your eye, before the water, before the rock, before the famous name in the box at the corner. Near the center of this tall sheet stands a brown horse, side-on, patient the way horses are when they are being handled, and around its legs two people are at work. One holds the animal, a groom keeping it steady. The other has crouched right down at its hooves in the shallows, low to the running water, scrubbing. It is the most ordinary scene imaginable, a chore you could have watched on any country river in old Japan: somebody washing the mud off a horse at the end of a day. Hold that humble little knot of labor in the middle of the picture. Everything else in the sheet, the cascade, the great rock, the legendary name, is arranged around these few working figures, and the gap between what they are doing and what this place is _called_ is the whole secret of the chapter.

So let us look, slowly, and find the water last, because the water here is a surprise and I do not want to spoil it before you have stood in front of the sheet.

### 12.1 Where to find a good Horse-washing Falls

Search for _Hokusai Yoshitsune Horse-washing Falls Yoshino Met_ on Google Arts and Culture, or open the Metropolitan Museum of Art's open-access page at metmuseum.org and call up the accession number **JP1084**. That is the impression in front of me as I write, a sheet that came into the Met through the Rogers Fund in 1936 (as of 2026-06, normally viewable on its open-access page; these prints are light-sensitive and rotated off physical display, so confirm before you travel). On Wikimedia Commons the same scan files under MET DP141250. The Met holds a second impression too, JP2927, so if you find the museum showing you a slightly different sheet under the same title, that is why. The Museum of Fine Arts in Boston, the Art Institute of Chicago, the Harvard Art Museums, and the Los Angeles County Museum of Art all hold this design as well (as of 2026-06). Open one full size and stand it up beside this page.

The full title printed in the cartouche at the upper left is _Washū Yoshino Yoshitsune uma-arai no taki_ (和州吉野義経馬洗滝), which means "Yoshitsune's Horse-washing Falls at Yoshino in Yamato Province." It belongs, like the Amida Falls and the Kirifuri Falls of the last two chapters, to _A Tour of the Waterfalls of the Provinces_ (_Shokoku taki meguri_ 諸国瀧廻り), the eight-print series published by Eijudō around 1833 to 1834, the same series, same publisher, same moment, same tall upright format. This is the third waterfall we have met, and the last we will meet, and you will see in a moment why the trio belongs together.

One small thing to clear up before we go on, because it appears in older books. The province is **Yamato** , written _Washū_ (和州) at the head of the title, the old province south of Nara that holds the Yoshino highlands. A few early catalogues mislabel it "Izumi," the neighboring province. They are wrong. The falls is at Yoshino, in Yamato, and the title says so plainly.

### 12.2 The water that comes down in steps

Now find the water, and prepare to have an expectation overturned.

After the Amida Falls and the Kirifuri Falls you may be braced for another vertical plunge, a column or a fan of threads dropping the full height of the sheet. This one does not do that. Look to the upper left, above and behind the horse, and follow the cascade as it descends. It does not pour straight down in a single drop. It comes down in _stages_ , a broad sheet of blue-and-white water sliding over one shelf of rock, gathering, then sliding over the next, then the next, stepping its way down the slope like water spilling down a flight of shallow stairs. It is wide, not narrow. It is terraced, not sheer. The white of the water spreads sideways across each step before it tips over the edge to the step below, so that the whole fall reads as a series of broad bright bands stacked one above another, blue and white, descending gently from the upper left toward the pool where the horse stands.

This is a completely different water from the two we have just studied. The Amida Falls fell as a single gathered column; the Kirifuri Falls fell as a forking fan of threads; this one falls as a stepped terrace, a broad cascade descending in sheets and stages. We will gather all three together at the end of the chapter, because the trio teaches something no single sheet can.

As in the rest of this series, the water is built on the Prussian blue, the _bero-ai_ you met under the wave in Chapter 1, with the brightest, most rushing parts left as bare white paper, unprinted, exactly as the foam of the wave and the column of the Amida Falls were bare paper. The printer inked the blue of the water and the dark of the rock and left the leaping white untouched. Where your impression's blue is deep and fresh, those terraced bands read as crisp ribbons of blue and white stacked down the slope; where it has faded or been lightly pulled, the steps read softer and greyer. We will come back to which sheet you hold.

### 12.3 The rock that splits the picture

Before we return to the horse, take in the thing that dominates the right half of the sheet and gives the whole composition its strange, lopsided power.

To the right of center, rising up and pressing toward the middle of the picture, is a great mass of rock, drawn in warm red-browns rather than the cool blue of the water. It is large, dramatic, almost a presence, a brown-red cliff or boulder-wall that bulges into the frame and divides the sheet in two. On one side, the upper left, the cool stepped water comes sliding down. On the other side stands this hot red-brown stone. The picture is split between them, water and rock, blue and red-brown, and the horse and its washers occupy the contested ground in the middle, in the shallows at the foot of both.

Let your eye feel that division. The rock does real work here. It blocks part of the view, it throws the water off to one side, and its warm color sets off the cool cascade the way the ochre knobs set off the blue at Kirifuri, except that here the warm stone is not a scattering of small footholds but one big commanding mass. It is the kind of bold, simplifying shape Hokusai loved, a single large form that organizes everything around it. Find where the rock's edge meets the water and you will see the seam along which he has built the whole design.

Now look down, to the foreground, at the floor of the picture. The water that has come down all those steps runs out across a pale shelf of rock at the bottom of the sheet, a shallow flat where the horse stands. Across that pale shelf, look for a scatter of small stippled blue dots, little flecks speckled over the surface. They read as foam, or as wet pebbles, or as the broken sparkle of shallow water running over stone, the picture is not precise and does not need to be. What they give you is texture, the sense of a real shallow streambed underfoot, water thin enough to wash a horse's legs in. That stippling is a quiet, almost throwaway touch, and most people never notice it. Find it, and the foreground stops being blank paper and becomes wet ground.

### 12.4 The hero who is not in the picture

Now we come to the name, and to the gap that is the heart of this sheet.

The falls is called Yoshitsune's Horse-washing Falls, and Yoshitsune was not a horse-washer. Minamoto no Yoshitsune (1159 to 1189) is one of the most famous figures in all of Japanese history and legend, a brilliant young warrior, the dazzling general who won the great battles that destroyed the rival Taira clan, and then the tragic hero who was turned on by his own jealous half-brother, the new ruler, and hunted to his death. He is Japan's beautiful doomed champion, the subject of endless plays and tales and prints, the sort of name every viewer of this sheet in old Edo would have known the way you know a national legend. And the story attached to _this_ place is that the fleeing Yoshitsune, hiding from his enemies in the wild Yoshino highlands during his last desperate years, washed his horse at this very fall. The name carries the legend. To say "Yoshitsune's Horse-washing Falls" is to say "this is the spot where the hero, on the run, paused to tend his mount."

So here is the thing to see, and it is the kind of quiet decision that tells you most about Hokusai. The hero is not in the picture. The figures washing the horse are not Yoshitsune. They are ordinary people, plain country commoners doing a plain country job. Hokusai had the perfect excuse to paint the famous warrior, the armor, the romance, the tragedy, the moment a legend touched this rock. Every other artist's instinct would have been to give you the hero. Hokusai gives you the chore. He paints the place as it is on any ordinary afternoon, with nameless people doing the humble work that the place is named for, and he lets the legend live entirely in the _name_ and the _ground_ , never appearing in the flesh.

This is worth holding onto, because it is the deepest of his conspiratorial moves, the reason this chapter exists at all. By now you have learned to read his titles, so you may already be bracing for the trick: twice in this series Hokusai has folded a meaning into a place-name and let the picture answer it sideways. At the Amida Falls the name was a Buddha's name, and he answered it by shaping the water into a halo. At Kirifuri the name meant "falling mist," and he answered it by drawing the water breaking apart into ever-finer threads on its way to becoming spray. So you come to this third sheet expecting the picture to act out its name, and instead it does the opposite. The name carries an entire human legend, a famous warrior and his tragic flight, and Hokusai answers it not by illustrating the legend but by _withholding_ it, painting only the ordinary life that goes on at a spot heavy with history. That restraint, the refusal to paint the obvious hero, is the most sophisticated version of the place-name move in the whole series, and it is hiding in plain sight under a scene so humble most viewers glance past it.

Let the two layers settle on top of each other. The surface layer is what your eye sees: a brown horse, two workers, a stepped fall, a red rock, shallow water over a pale shelf, an everyday scene with almost nothing happening. The hidden layer is what the name supplies: seven hundred years ago a hunted hero crouched at this same water with his own horse, in fear for his life, and the place has carried his name ever since. A nameless fall where commoners wash a horse is not worth a sheet; _this_ fall is worth a sheet because of who is said to have stood here, and Hokusai trusts you to bring that knowledge with you and lay it over the humble scene yourself. He does not lecture you with the hero. He names the place, paints the present day, and lets the past hum underneath. The commoner washing the horse stands exactly where the legend stands, doing exactly what the legend did, and the only difference between the everyday and the heroic is the name written in the box at the corner. History here is not a figure in armor. It is a layer in the ground, folded into a word.

### 12.5 Three waters, one hand

This is the third and last of the waterfalls, so before we leave it, open all three on your screen at once if you can, the Amida Falls from Chapter 10, the Kirifuri Falls from Chapter 11, and this Horse-washing Falls. Set them side by side and look at nothing but the water in each. This is the comparison the trio was built for, and it pays off best seen whole.

At the Amida Falls the water is a _halo and a column_ : it begins as a near-circular pool high at the top, ringed like the disc of light behind a Buddha's head, and falls from it as one gathered band of white that narrows as it drops. The motion is round at the source and vertical in the fall, water pulling together.

At the Kirifuri Falls the water is a _branching fan_ : it enters as a few narrow streams and immediately forks, splitting again and again into a spreading root-system of slender white veins, flying apart wider and wider down the dark cliff, water coming undone.

Here at the Horse-washing Falls the water is a _terraced staircase_ : a broad sheet sliding down in stages, spreading sideways across one shelf of rock and tipping over to the next, descending gently in stacked bands, water stepping down.

Round, branching, stepped. A column that gathers, a fan that splays, a terrace that descends in stages. Three sheets, made by one man for one series in one year, on one subject, falling water, and each one invents a wholly different structure for the same substance. Hokusai was not copying how three particular waterfalls happened to look. He was sitting down, three times over, with the single hardest thing in nature to draw, moving water, and building a fresh visual system for it each time: the halo, the roots, the steps. He could have repeated himself and nobody would have minded. Instead he treated each fall as a new problem in design and solved it a new way. By the end of these three chapters you should be able to recognize this about him on sight: that his real subject was never the thing itself but the _invention_ of how to draw it, and that water, which never holds still and never holds a shape, was the perfect material on which to show the range of a hand that could give it three shapes in a year.
